## Management Summary

### Verdict

🔴 **Critical security concerns** - This web shop lets unauthenticated attackers take over administrator accounts and run code on the server. It is acceptable only as an isolated training target and must never hold real customer or payment data.

**Risk distribution:** 🔴 Critical: 4 · 🟠 High: 29 · 🟡 Medium: 46 · 🟢 Low: n/a · **Total: 79**<br/>**Findings register:** 162 · 🔴 Critical: 5 · 🟠 High: 61 · 🟡 Medium: 96 - the distribution above counts insecure-practice findings under their weakness; see [§8 Findings Register](#8-findings-register)<br/>**Assessment evidence:** 54 of 77 finding(s) confirmed in code<br/>**Weakness classes:** 42 (35 implementation, 7 design) - see [§7 Weakness Register](#7-weakness-register)

<br/>

**Security concerns behind this assessment:**

<blockquote style="border-left: 3px solid #dc2626; padding: 16px 20px; margin: 0;">

- **Accounts taken over without a password** — SQL injection in login and a password derived from the public email address let anonymous attackers sign in as any user, including administrators. *(🔴 [F-010](#f-010), 🔴 [F-004](#f-004) → [W-002](#w-002))*
- **Customer data read straight from the database** — SQL injection in product search lets anyone without an account dump stored user records, including emails and password hashes. *(🔴 [F-005](#f-005) → [W-002](#w-002))*
- **Server takeover by a registered user** — Server-side code injection lets any registered customer run commands on the web server through a crafted profile name. *(🔴 [F-011](#f-011) → [W-001](#w-001))* — ✓ cited finding in a code-verified chain
- **Forged logins for any user or role** — The signing key for login tokens sits in the source code, so anyone who reads it can mint valid sessions for any user. *(🟠 [F-022](#f-022) → [W-010](#w-010))* — ✓ cited finding in a code-verified chain
- **Anyone can register as administrator** — Mass assignment on account creation accepts a client-supplied role, so an anonymous visitor becomes an administrator at sign-up. *(🟠 [F-071](#f-071), 🟠 [F-072](#f-072) → [W-006](#w-006))* — ✓ cited finding in a code-verified chain
- **Other customers' records read and changed** — Missing ownership checks (IDOR) let any signed-in customer read or change records that belong to other customers. *(🔴 [F-001](#f-001) → [W-005](#w-005))* — left open by the evidence review: 🔴 [F-001](#f-001) — Attacker-Controlled Owner ID Authorization Bypass

</blockquote>

<br/>

Until these paths are closed, treat the system as fully exposed to anyone who can reach it and keep it away from real users and data.

**About this assessment:** An AI-assisted threat model derived from the implementation of Juice Shop. It reconstructs the implemented architecture (9 components and 6 external services, see [§2](#2-architecture-diagrams)) and identifies threats and control gaps in it.


**Method and limits:** Automated static analysis of code and configuration, not a pentest or a team threat-modeling session; Low and Informational findings not reported (threshold: medium) - see [§11 Out of Scope](#11-out-of-scope).

### Remediation Priorities

The root causes behind the findings, ranked by severity, each with the measures to do first. The lasting fix under a root cause removes it and every finding it causes. Every mitigation that fixes a Critical finding is listed. Details in the [§7 Weakness Register](#7-weakness-register) and the [§10 Mitigation Register](#10-mitigation-register).

### Open Questions for the Team

The code cannot answer these questions; the people who own the business and the deployment can. Each question states under it what its answer changes.

- _How critical are User credentials and accounts and Authentication session tokens to the business, and what harm would their disclosure, manipulation or unavailability cause?_
  The answer weights the impact rating and fix order of every finding that reaches these assets.
- _Which sensitive business records can the production database account used by Juice Shop Express API or Authentication & Session Surface access?_ ([W-002](#w-002): [F-005](#f-005), [F-010](#f-010))
  Personal or regulated data in reach raises both the breach impact and any notification duty; a narrowly scoped account limits the blast radius.
- _If an attacker runs code in Juice Shop Express API, which other services, secrets or credentials can that process reach?_ ([F-011](#f-011), [F-062](#f-062) (unproven))
  Every reachable service or credential extends this from one component to a wider compromise and raises the priority of isolating that process; an isolated process contains it.

### Security Posture & Top Threats

**Figure 1a - Runtime Architecture and Threat Overview**

At runtime, Juice Shop has 8 components in 3 layers and exchanges data with 6 external services. Figure 1a shows these data flows and where each of the 6 attack scenarios below begins. The complete boundary catalogue remains in [§1 Trust Boundaries](#trust-boundaries).

<picture><source media="(prefers-color-scheme: dark)" srcset="security-assessment-juice-shop-thorough-v0.1.0.figure1-dark.svg"><img src="security-assessment-juice-shop-thorough-v0.1.0.figure1.svg" alt="Figure 1a - Runtime Architecture and Threat Overview"></picture>

**Build pipeline**, not drawn in this runtime view: GitHub Actions, GitLab CI. ⑦ A2 · Supply-Chain / Build Attacker → [Figure 1b](#figure-1b).

Role access and finding assignments are listed in [Identified Actors](#identified-actors).

**Actor grouping.** Regular self-registered users are grouped with anonymous internet attackers because registration is open. Repository readers are grouped with anonymous internet attackers because the source repository is public. Each finding retains its login and privilege requirements.

<a id="figure-1b"></a>

**Figure 1b - Supply Chain and Build**

Juice Shop builds in GitHub Actions, GitLab CI. Figure 1b shows the inputs, build systems and release artifacts the repository evidences, with 10 findings attached to them. The highlighted path follows 🟠 [F-025](#f-025) — Lockfile generation disabled by npm configuration — .npmrc:1 as far as the repository evidences it. The control assessment is in [§6.11](#611-operations-runtime-and-supply-chain-controls).

<picture><source media="(prefers-color-scheme: dark)" srcset="security-assessment-juice-shop-thorough-v0.1.0.figure1b-dark.svg"><img src="security-assessment-juice-shop-thorough-v0.1.0.figure1b.svg" alt="Figure 1b - Supply Chain and Build"></picture>

**Figure 2 - Attack Routes and Impact**

Each numbered route names one example finding from the corresponding Top Threats group with its access prerequisite, the register weakness it is linked to, and the group's potential business harm, which depends on deployment and affected assets. W-IDs in parentheses link the example to an existing weakness. The attack step, affected component and technical consequence are listed in the findings register. Red arrows indicate attacks; dashed red arrows involve a victim; grey arrows lead to potential harm.

<picture><source media="(prefers-color-scheme: dark)" srcset="security-assessment-juice-shop-thorough-v0.1.0.figure2-dark.svg"><img src="security-assessment-juice-shop-thorough-v0.1.0.figure2.svg" alt="Figure 2 - Actors, attack routes, weaknesses and impact"></picture>

**Threat actors.** The actors below drive the numbered attack paths in the figures above. The **Juice Shop User** is the *victim* of client-side attacks (XSS / CSRF), not an attacker. Figure 2 marks victim interaction on the applicable attack routes.

- **Juice Shop User** — legitimate end user; target of client-side attacks; target of ⑥ Output Encoding / Cross-Site Scripting.
- **Internet Attacker** — can self-register a regular account; drives ① Broken Authorization & Access Control, ② Insecure Query Construction & Data Access, ③ Hardcoded Secrets & Weak Cryptography, ④ Remote Code Execution (unsafe eval), ⑤ Sensitive File & Secret Exposure, ⑥ Output Encoding / Cross-Site Scripting.
- **Supply-Chain / Build Attacker** — compromises a dependency or build pipeline, or contributes malicious code to a public repo; drives ⑦ Build Pipeline & Dependency Integrity.

**7 structural threats**, grouped by weakness class - each row is one threat, not one finding. *Threat Description* states the general architectural weakness (STRIDE in brackets); *Findings* lists the concrete instances by severity under their component, each linked to the [§8 Findings Register](#8-findings-register), and on screen an ID shows its title when you point at it; *Risk & Impact* combines severity with business consequence; *Primary Measures* names the most important measures for this threat, most important first, one per root cause. All measures are in the [§10 Mitigation Register](#10-mitigation-register).

**Verified attack chains.** 8 fully viable ([AC-T-006](#ac-t-006), [AC-B-001](#ac-b-001), [AC-B-002](#ac-b-002), [AC-B-003](#ac-b-003), [AC-B-005](#ac-b-005), [AC-B-007](#ac-b-007), [AC-T-005](#ac-t-005), [AC-T-007](#ac-t-007)); 1 partially blocked ([AC-T-002](#ac-t-002)). These chains combine individual findings into end-to-end exploitation paths verified step-by-step against the code - see [§9 Abuse Cases](#9-abuse-cases) for the per-step breakdown and blocking mitigations.

### AI / LLM Exposure

This system embeds an LLM/AI surface (an LLM/AI integration); the risks below are architectural - they follow from how untrusted input reaches the model's prompt, tools, and outputs. See **[§6 Security Architecture](#6-security-architecture)** for the per-control detail.



- **[LLM01](https://genai.owasp.org/llmrisk/llm01/) Prompt Injection** — Untrusted user input reaches the LLM prompt/context without sufficient trust separation, letting an attacker override system instructions, redirect tool calls, or coerce unintended model behaviour. _([C-03](#c-03) — Juice Shop Express API, [C-08](#c-08) — Angular Client Application)_
  - ↳ 🟠 [F-036](#f-036) — Client-supplied chat history and roles passed to the model _([ASI01](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/))_
  - ↳ 🟠 [F-070](#f-070) — Chatbot coupon tool mints any discount _([ASI01](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/), [ASI02](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/))_
  - ↳ 🟠 [F-073](#f-073) — Coupon discount cap enforced only by prompt text _([ASI02](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/), [ASI01](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/))_
  - ↳ 🟠 [F-075](#f-075) — Chatbot coupon tool issues arbitrary discounts on model
  - ↳ 🟡 [F-103](#f-103) — Stored username and reviews enter model context as instructions _([ASI01](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/))_
  - ↳ 🟡 [F-110](#f-110) — Chat auto-sends attacker-supplied

- **[LLM05](https://genai.owasp.org/llmrisk/llm05/) Improper Output Handling** — LLM-generated output flows into a downstream sink (browser, shell, query, eval) without validation or encoding, turning a model completion into an injection vector. _([C-03](#c-03) — Juice Shop Express API)_
  - ↳ 🟠 [F-075](#f-075) — Chatbot coupon tool issues arbitrary discounts on model

- **[LLM06](https://genai.owasp.org/llmrisk/llm06/) Excessive Agency** — The LLM is granted tool/function-calling authority without a secondary authorization boundary, so a manipulated model turn can invoke privileged actions on the user's behalf. _([C-03](#c-03) — Juice Shop Express API)_
  - ↳ 🟠 [F-013](#f-013) — Chatbot identity taken from an unverified JWT _([ASI03](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/))_
  - ↳ 🟠 [F-070](#f-070) — Chatbot coupon tool mints any discount _([ASI01](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/), [ASI02](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/))_
  - ↳ 🟠 [F-073](#f-073) — Coupon discount cap enforced only by prompt text _([ASI02](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/), [ASI01](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/))_
  - ↳ 🟠 [F-075](#f-075) — Chatbot coupon tool issues arbitrary discounts on model

- **[LLM08](https://genai.owasp.org/llmrisk/llm08/) Vector and Embedding Weaknesses** — Retrieval/embedding inputs are not isolated by trust, letting injected documents or queries steer retrieval-augmented responses or leak indexed content. _([C-03](#c-03) — Juice Shop Express API)_
  - ↳ 🟠 [F-013](#f-013) — Chatbot identity taken from an unverified JWT _([ASI03](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/))_

- **[LLM07](https://genai.owasp.org/llmrisk/llm07/) System Prompt Leakage** — The system prompt — carrying internal policy, tool rules, or secrets — is extractable through conversational manipulation, exposing the tool capability surface and internal business logic encoded in it. _([C-03](#c-03) — Juice Shop Express API)_
  - ↳ 🟡 [F-122](#f-122) — Confidential discount rule embedded in extractable

- **[LLM10](https://genai.owasp.org/llmrisk/llm10/) Unbounded Consumption** — The LLM endpoint imposes no authentication, rate, or quota boundary, so any client can drive unbounded model invocations — uncontrolled provider cost and denial of service for legitimate users. _([C-03](#c-03) — Juice Shop Express API)_
  - ↳ 🟡 [F-143](#f-143) — Unbounded LLM consumption on unauthenticated chat endpoint
  - ↳ 🟡 [F-144](#f-144) — Unbounded unauthenticated LLM consumption on chat endpoint

### Operational Strengths

Operational controls rated Adequate or Partial - grouped into broad clusters (full per-control breakdown in [§6](#6-security-architecture)). Clusters demoted to Weak by open Critical/High findings appear in [§6](#6-security-architecture) instead, not here.

<colgroup><col width="18%" style="width:18%"><col width="32%" style="width:32%"><col width="14%" style="width:14%"><col width="36%" style="width:36%"></colgroup>
<thead><tr><th>Strength</th><th>What's in Place</th><th>Effectiveness</th><th>Open gaps</th></tr></thead>
<tbody>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><strong>Container &amp; Supply-Chain Hardening</strong></td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><em>Build-time and runtime hardening - minimal base image, non-root execution, dependency inventory.</em><br/>Release and container image build hardening</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">✅ Adequate</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">-</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><strong>Hardened HTTP Stack</strong></td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><em>Browser-facing HTTP hardening — security headers, cookie flags, cross-origin policy, and abuse-protection limits.</em><br/>WebSocket CORS origin restriction<br/>Rate limiting and access logging</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">⚠️ Partial</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">4 Medium findings remain open:<br/>🟡 <a href="#f-084">F-084</a> — OAuth implicit flow without state, nonce or PKCE<br/>🟡 <a href="#f-096">F-096</a> — Data erasure request without re-verification<br/>🟡 <a href="#f-097">F-097</a> — Cross-site request forgery on profile<br/>🟡 <a href="#f-110">F-110</a> — Chat auto-sends attacker-supplied</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><strong>Observability &amp; Audit</strong></td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><em>Runtime visibility - access logging, audit trails, and operational telemetry for post-incident review.</em><br/>Error handling and logging for WebSocket provider failures</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">⚠️ Partial</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">-</td></tr>
</tbody>
</table>


**Bottom line:** These controls narrow specific attack surfaces but none eliminates a Critical finding on its own.

## Critical Findings by Root Cause

4 of 5 Critical findings trace back to 3 root causes; fixing a root cause addresses every finding linked to it. 1 has no root cause linked yet.

    T010["F-010 - SQL injection in login query through the email field<br/>login.ts"]:::leaf

**Structural fixes** (full detail in [§7 Weakness Register](#7-weakness-register)):

- **[W-001](#w-001)** — Replace evaluation of application values with a data parser or a fixed set of operations whose arguments remain data _(closes [F-011](#f-011))_
- **[W-002](#w-002)** — Provide one parameterised repository or query-builder path and prohibit application-value interpolation in database queries _(closes [F-005](#f-005), [F-010](#f-010))_
- **[W-005](#w-005)** — Enforce authorization through a shared server-side policy layer and make ownership and tenant scope mandatory inputs to data access _(closes [F-001](#f-001))_
- **No root cause linked** — [F-004](#f-004): see the [§8 Findings Register](#8-findings-register)

## 1. System Overview

Probably the most modern and sophisticated insecure web application

**Repository:** `https://github.com/juice-shop/juice-shop.git`
**Runtime:** Node\.js 22 - 26

### Scope

This threat model covers 9 components of juice-shop: 

- Sequelize application data access
- CI/CD Pipeline
- Juice Shop Express API
- Web3 / Wallet / NFT Surface
- marsdb Embedded Store
- Real-time WebSocket Channel
- SQLite Embedded Database
- Angular Client Application
- Authentication & Session Surface

**Not individually examined:** 1 source file in 1 component had no route, lead or security signal (CI/CD Pipeline 1).

**Not analysed:** 92 TypeScript files the build leaves out, so their routes and code sites were not checked (`data/static/codefixes/accessLogDisclosureChallenge_1_correct.ts`, `data/static/codefixes/accessLogDisclosureChallenge_2.ts`, `data/static/codefixes/accessLogDisclosureChallenge_3.ts`, `data/static/codefixes/accessLogDisclosureChallenge_4.ts`, `data/static/codefixes/adminSectionChallenge_1_correct.ts`, `data/static/codefixes/adminSectionChallenge_2.ts`, `data/static/codefixes/adminSectionChallenge_3.ts`, `data/static/codefixes/adminSectionChallenge_4.ts`, `data/static/codefixes/changeProductChallenge_1.ts`, `data/static/codefixes/changeProductChallenge_2.ts` and 82 more).

**Not assessed:** 1 security-relevant code location was read but could not be judged (Juice Shop Express API 1); `security-assessment.yaml` records each with its reason under `meta.undecided_targets`.

**Not assigned to any component:** 5 program files belong to no component and were not analysed (`frontend/` 5).

**Out of scope:** third-party hosted dependencies, browser runtime, operating-system kernel, and the underlying network infrastructure.

**Basis:** An automated, AI-assisted threat model built from the repository's source code and configuration - static analysis only, no dynamic or penetration testing. It describes the system as built, not as designed.

---

<a id="identified-actors"></a>
### Identified Actors

Figure 1a draws **Internet Attacker** as attacker and **Juice Shop User** and **Juice Shop Admin** as legitimate roles of Juice Shop. [Figure 1b](#figure-1b) draws **Supply-Chain / Build Attacker** and the build it attacks. Each row gives that actor's access and the findings attributed to it.

| Actor | Type | Access | Findings |
|----------------------|---------------|----------------------|-----------------|
| A1 · Internet Attacker | Attacker | Can self-register a regular account | 🔴 5 · 🟠 56 · 🟡 82 |
| A2 · Supply-Chain / Build Attacker | Attacker | Compromises a dependency or build pipeline,<br/>or contributes malicious code to a public<br/>repo | 🟠 2 · 🟡 8 |
| Juice Shop User | Role (victim) | Person using the client application,<br/>evidenced by its browser entry point. | - |
| Juice Shop Admin | Privileged role | Application role with elevated rights. | - |

9 findings (3 High, 6 Medium) have no assigned actor. That does not mean they are out of reach.

*🔴 **Unsafe**: relied upon but defeated or trivially bypassable - fix it · 🔴 **Missing**: never built - add it · 🟠 **Weak**: present, with exploitable gaps · 🟡 **Partial**: covers some surfaces, meaningful gaps remain · 🟢 **Adequate**: present and sound. A control counts for the component whose paths hold its implementation evidence. A finding counts for every component it affects, so the columns can add up to more than the register. [§6](#6-security-architecture) holds the full assessment.*

**Key takeaway:** 1 component has at least one control rated Unsafe or Missing (C-08); 5 components have no component-specific control (C-03, C-04, C-05, C-06, C-09); 29 controls apply system-wide.

> **Legend:** `-->` synchronous request/response (REST, HTTPS, gRPC) · **red border** ≥ 3 Critical threats on the component · **amber border** ≥ 2 High threats

## 3. Attack Walkthroughs

This section walks through how the highest-risk findings are exploited - one short walkthrough per Critical, each with attack steps, a focused sequence diagram, and the primary mitigation. Which root cause each Critical traces back to is in [Critical Findings by Root Cause](#critical-findings-by-root-cause); full per-finding context (severity rationale, assets, detection signals) is in the [§8 Findings Register](#8-findings-register).

### 3.1 Server-side code execution in User Profile

**Source:** 🔴 [F-011](#f-011) — `routes/userProfile.ts:61`

Severity 🔴 Critical ([CWE-95](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/95.html)). STRIDE: Elevation of Privilege. See [§8 F-011](#f-011) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. I register and log in, then POST `/profile` with username=#{`global.process.mainModule.require('child_process').execSync('id').toString()`}.
2. I request GET `/profile`, and the server evals my username and runs the OS command.
3. The command output is rendered as my username, which confirms remote code execution on the API host.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant App
    Note over App: Juice Shop Express API - routes/userProfile.ts:61
    Attacker->>App: The attacker sends a crafted request targeting routes/userProfile.ts line 61
    App->>App: Server-side code execution
    App-->>Attacker: The attacker reads the response confirming the weakness - CWE-95
    alt Current state — F-011
        Attacker->>App: The attacker sends the exploit for F-011
        App-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-187 — Remove server-side evaluation of untrusted input (F-011)
        Attacker->>App: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        App-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-187](#m-187) (Remove server-side evaluation of untrusted input (🔴 [F-011](#f-011))) lands, 🔴 [F-011](#f-011) — Server-side code execution is exploitable at `routes/userProfile.ts:61` (Critical-severity, [CWE-95](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/95.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-187](#m-187) (Remove server-side evaluation of untrusted input (🔴 [F-011](#f-011)))

### 3.2 Attacker-Controlled Owner ID Authorization Bypass in Memory

**Source:** 🔴 [F-001](#f-001) — `routes/memory.ts:15`

Severity 🔴 Critical ([CWE-639](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/639.html)). STRIDE: Tampering. See [§8 F-001](#f-001) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. An attacker crafts a request that reaches the code at `routes/memory.ts:15`.
2. A signed-in attacker puts another user's ID into the owner field of the request (`req.body.UserId` and the like); the query trusts it instead of the session identity, so the attacker reads or changes every other user's records - horizontal privilege escalation across all accounts.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant App
    Note over App: Juice Shop Express API - routes/memory.ts:15
    Attacker->>App: The attacker sends a crafted request targeting routes/memory.ts line 15
    App->>App: Attacker-Controlled Owner ID Authorization Bypass
    App-->>Attacker: The attacker reads the response confirming the weakness - CWE-639
    alt Current state — F-001
        Attacker->>App: The attacker sends the exploit for F-001
        App-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-181 — Enforce object-level (ownership) authorization (F-001)
        Attacker->>App: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        App-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-181](#m-181) (Enforce object-level (ownership) authorization (🔴 [F-001](#f-001))) lands, 🔴 [F-001](#f-001) — Attacker-Controlled Owner ID Authorization Bypass is exploitable at `routes/memory.ts:15` (Critical-severity, [CWE-639](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/639.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-181](#m-181) (Enforce object-level (ownership) authorization (🔴 [F-001](#f-001)))
- Defence in depth: ◑ [M-165](#m-165) (Manual review: verify Attacker-Controlled Owner ID Authorization Bypass)

### 3.3 SQL injection in product search query

**Source:** 🔴 [F-005](#f-005) — `routes/search.ts:23`

Severity 🔴 Critical ([CWE-89](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/89.html)). STRIDE: Tampering. See [§8 F-005](#f-005) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. I send GET `/rest/products/search?q=qwert``')) UNION SELECT id,email,password,'`4','5','6','7','8','9' FROM Users-- with no login.
2. The server concatenates my payload into the raw SQL and returns the user emails and password hashes as product rows.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant API
    participant DB
    Note over API: Juice Shop Express API - routes/search.ts:23
    Note over DB: Database
    Attacker->>API: GET /rest/products/search with classical OR 1=1 payload
    API->>DB: SQL built by string interpolation - payload becomes query
    DB-->>API: First matching row regardless of intended predicate
    API-->>Attacker: 200 OK with authenticated session / leaked rows
    alt Current state — F-005
        Attacker->>API: The attacker sends the exploit for F-005
        API-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-185 — Use parameterized replacements in search query
        Attacker->>API: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        API-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-185](#m-185) (Use parameterized replacements in search query) lands, 🔴 [F-005](#f-005) — SQL injection in product search query is exploitable at `routes/search.ts:23` (Critical-severity, [CWE-89](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/89.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-185](#m-185) (Use parameterized replacements in search query)

### 3.4 SQL injection in login query

**Source:** 🔴 [F-010](#f-010) — `routes/login.ts:34`

Severity 🔴 Critical ([CWE-89](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/89.html)). STRIDE: Tampering. See [§8 F-010](#f-010) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. I send POST `/rest/user/login` with email set to admin@juice-`sh.op`'-- and any password.
2. The quote closes the email literal and the comment drops the password predicate, so the query returns the admin row.
3. The handler signs a JWT for that row via `security.authorize` and returns it, giving me an admin session.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant API
    participant DB
    Note over API: Authentication & Session Surface - routes/login.ts:34
    Note over DB: Database
    Attacker->>API: Crafted HTTP request to the affected endpoint with classical OR 1=1 payload
    API->>DB: SQL built by string interpolation - payload becomes query
    DB-->>API: First matching row regardless of intended predicate
    API-->>Attacker: 200 OK with authenticated session / leaked rows
    alt Current state — F-010
        Attacker->>API: The attacker sends the exploit for F-010
        API-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-186 — Use parameterized queries for login lookup
        Attacker->>API: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        API-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-186](#m-186) (Use parameterized queries for login lookup) lands, 🔴 [F-010](#f-010) — SQL injection in login query through the email field is exploitable at `routes/login.ts:34` (Critical-severity, [CWE-89](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/89.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-186](#m-186) (Use parameterized queries for login lookup)

### 3.5 OAuth account password derived from public email address

**Source:** 🔴 [F-004](#f-004) — `frontend/src/app/oauth/oauth.component.ts:30`

Severity 🔴 Critical ([CWE-1391](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/1391.html)). STRIDE: Spoofing. See [§8 F-004](#f-004) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. I find a target's email address that is linked to Google sign-in, for example from reviews or other public profile data.
2. I reverse the email string and base64-encode it, which gives the exact password the client registered for that account.
3. I POST the email and derived password to `/rest/user/login` and receive a valid JWT for the victim's account.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant App
    Note over App: Angular Client Application - frontend/src/app/oauth/oauth.component.ts:30
    Attacker->>App: The attacker sends a crafted request targeting frontend/src/app/oauth/oauth.component.ts line 30
    App->>App: OAuth account password derived from public email address
    App-->>Attacker: The attacker reads the response confirming the weakness - CWE-1391
    alt Current state — F-004
        Attacker->>App: The attacker sends the exploit for F-004
        App-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-184 — Exchange OAuth tokens server-side instead of derived passwor
        Attacker->>App: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        App-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-184](#m-184) (Exchange OAuth tokens server-side instead of derived passwords) lands, 🔴 [F-004](#f-004) — OAuth account password derived from public email address is exploitable at `frontend/src/app/oauth/oauth.component.ts:30` (Critical-severity, [CWE-1391](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/1391.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-184](#m-184) (Exchange OAuth tokens server-side instead of derived passwords)

### 3.6 Self-registration can set the admin role in Juice Shop Express API

**Source:** 🟠 [F-072](#f-072) — `server.ts:501`

Severity 🟠 High ([CWE-915](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/915.html)). STRIDE: Elevation of Privilege. See [§8 F-072](#f-072) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. Send POST `/api/Users` without authentication, with role set to admin in the JSON body.
2. Finale create binds all body attributes; the `User.role` setter stores 'admin' (`isIn` allows it).
3. Log in with the new account and receive a token whose role is admin.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant App
    Note over App: Juice Shop Express API - server.ts:501
    Attacker->>App: The attacker sends a crafted request targeting server.ts line 501
    App->>App: Self-registration can set the admin role through the request
    App-->>Attacker: The attacker reads the response confirming the weakness - CWE-915
    alt Current state — F-072
        Attacker->>App: The attacker sends the exploit for F-072
        App-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-238 — Strip or server-assign role on user registration
        Attacker->>App: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        App-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-238](#m-238) (Strip or server-assign role on user registration) lands, 🟠 [F-072](#f-072) — Self-registration can set the admin role through the request is exploitable at `server.ts:501` (High-severity, [CWE-915](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/915.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-238](#m-238) (Strip or server-assign role on user registration)

### 3.7 Hardcoded RSA private key used to sign session JWTs

**Source:** 🟠 [F-022](#f-022) — `lib/insecurity.ts:21`

Severity 🟠 High ([CWE-798](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/798.html)). STRIDE: Spoofing. See [§8 F-022](#f-022) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. The RSA private key that signs session JWTs is a source literal at `lib/insecurity.ts:21`.
2. Anyone who obtains the source, for example from a public repository, an image or a leaked build, can use the key to sign arbitrary JWTs with any user id and role, such as admin. `isAuthorized()` accepts these tokens because they verify against the matching public key.
3. Tokens are `RS256`-signed with a 6h expiry (line 54), which does not stop forged tokens.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant App
    Note over App: Authentication & Session Surface - lib/insecurity.ts:21
    Attacker->>App: The attacker sends a crafted request targeting lib/insecurity.ts line 21
    App->>App: Hardcoded RSA private key used to sign session JWTs
    App-->>Attacker: The attacker reads the response confirming the weakness - CWE-798
    alt Current state — F-022
        Attacker->>App: The attacker sends the exploit for F-022
        App-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-194 — Load JWT signing key from a secret store
        Attacker->>App: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        App-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-194](#m-194) (Load JWT signing key from a secret store) lands, 🟠 [F-022](#f-022) — Hardcoded RSA private key used to sign session JWTs is exploitable at `lib/insecurity.ts:21` (High-severity, [CWE-798](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/798.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-194](#m-194) (Load JWT signing key from a secret store)

### 3.8 Self-registration accepts a client-supplied admin role

**Source:** 🟠 [F-071](#f-071) — `server.ts:408`

Severity 🟠 High ([CWE-915](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/915.html)). STRIDE: Elevation of Privilege. See [§8 F-071](#f-071) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. POST `/api/Users` with JSON `{"email":"x@y.z","password":"p","passwordRepeat":"p","role":"admin"}`.
2. The finale create persists `role=admin`.
3. Log in as the new user and receive a JWT carrying role admin.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant App
    Note over App: Juice Shop Express API - server.ts:408
    Attacker->>App: The attacker sends a crafted request targeting server.ts line 408
    App->>App: Self-registration accepts a client-supplied admin role
    App-->>Attacker: The attacker reads the response confirming the weakness - CWE-915
    alt Current state — F-071
        Attacker->>App: The attacker sends the exploit for F-071
        App-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-237 — Strip privileged fields from registration requests
        Attacker->>App: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        App-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-237](#m-237) (Strip privileged fields from registration requests) lands, 🟠 [F-071](#f-071) — Self-registration accepts a client-supplied admin role is exploitable at `server.ts:408` (High-severity, [CWE-915](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/915.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-237](#m-237) (Strip privileged fields from registration requests)

## 6. Security Architecture

This chapter is organized by security-control category. Findings are listed only where the affected control is described.

_Cataloged controls: 39 total - 3 adequate, 13 partial, 13 weak, 5 unsafe, 5 missing._

**How to read the verdicts.** Every control category (and every sub-control below it) carries exactly one status. The two red verdicts do **not** mean the same thing - this is the distinction that decides what you have to do about a finding:

| Status | Meaning | What to do |
|----------|------------------------------------|----------------------|
| 🔴 Unsafe | Present and relied upon, but defeated or<br/>trivially bypassable | Fix the existing control |
| 🔴 Missing | Control was never built | Add the control |
| 🟠 Weak | Present, but has exploitable gaps | Strengthen it |
| 🟡 Partial | Present, but with meaningful gaps | Close the gap |
| 🟢 Adequate | Control is present and sound | Nothing, keep it |

So "🔴 Unsafe" on a control category does *not* mean the control is absent - it means the control exists but does not hold (e.g. an `MD5` password hash, a raw-SQL query path, a hardcoded signing key). "🔴 Missing" is reserved for controls that were never built (e.g. no `Content-Security-Policy` header).

### 6.2 Identity and Authentication Controls

<a id="ctrl-identity-and-authentication-controls"></a>


**Systemic weaknesses:** [W-003](#w-003), [W-011](#w-011), [W-012](#w-012), [W-013](#w-013)
**Verdict:** 🔴 Unsafe - the password flows exist but are defeated by SQL injection, guessable security answers and a client-settable admin role.

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.2.1 OAuth implicit flow with redirect allow-list](#621-oauth-implicit-flow-with-redirect-allow-list)
- [6.2.2 Password change transport](#622-password-change-transport)
- [6.2.3 Two-factor authentication step-up at login](#623-two-factor-authentication-step-up-at-login)
- [6.2.4 User Registration](#624-user-registration)
- [6.2.5 Password Reset](#625-password-reset)

**Implemented controls:** OAuth implicit flow with redirect allow-list, Password change transport, Hardcoded test credentials in client, Two-factor authentication step-up at login, User Registration.

**Assessment:** The knowledge factor is where authentication breaks: SQL injection in login (🔴 [F-010](#f-010)), an OAuth password derived from the public email (🔴 [F-004](#f-004)), and reset by security answer (🟠 [F-020](#f-020), 🟠 [F-021](#f-021)). TOTP holds only partly. Each successful flow above terminates in the server issuing a session token; the signing, validation, storage and lifecycle of that token are described in [§6.3 Session and Token Controls](#63-session-and-token-controls).

<!-- §6.2 AUTH-MECHANISMS-FROZEN END -->

**Findings in this category without a control:**

- 🟠 [F-013](#f-013) — Chatbot identity taken from an unverified JWT
- 🟠 [F-014](#f-014) — JWT verify call missing algorithms allowlist
- 🟠 [F-015](#f-015) — Password change skips current password check
- 🟠 [F-019](#f-019) — JWT verification does not pin the accepted signature
- 🟠 [F-047](#f-047) — Passwords stored and compared as unsalted MD5
- 🟠 [F-056](#f-056) — Passwords hashed and compared as unsalted MD5
- 🟠 [F-076](#f-076) — JWT verification does not pin the expected algorithm
- 🟡 [F-078](#f-078) — Review author taken from request body
- 🟡 [F-081](#f-081) — Wallet ownership accepted from request body
- 🟡 [F-083](#f-083) — OAuth login trusts userinfo without checking token audience
- 🟡 [F-086](#f-086) — Token checks omit issuer and audience, and the jws path
- 🟡 [F-113](#f-113) — Security answers hashed with a fast HMAC
- 🟡 [F-132](#f-132) — Unauthenticated WebSocket Channel

<a id="websocket-connection-authentication"></a>
**WebSocket connection authentication.**


**Status:** 🔴 Missing - no handshake authentication exists, so any client receives stored notifications.

This control is not evidenced in the repository.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): The connection handler accepts any client without token, session or handshake authentication, and immediately replays all stored notifications (including challenge flags) to it. -->
The real-time channel has no identity check at connection time:

- Any client can connect and immediately receive every stored notification, including challenge flags (🟡 [F-132](#f-132)).
- Socket messages are untrusted input from unauthenticated peers, so handler weaknesses are reachable by anyone (🟡 [F-149](#f-149)).

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="oauth-implicit-flow-with-redirect-allow-list"></a>
#### 6.2.1 OAuth implicit flow with redirect allow-list

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - a redirect allow-list is checked, but the implicit flow has no state or PKCE.

<!-- Model implementation note (facts for the intro): frontend/src/app/login/login.component.ts:80-87,148; user.service.ts:68 -->
`login.component.ts` builds the authorize URL after checking the redirect URI against a configured allow-list. `oauth.component.ts` reads the access token from the redirect fragment and `user.service.ts` calls the userinfo endpoint.

The diagram shows how the frontend OAuth adapter ends in the local login flow:
```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor User
    participant SPA as Angular Client
    participant IdP as OAuth Provider
    participant API as Local API
    User->>IdP: Authorize with response_type token
    IdP-->>SPA: Redirect with access token in fragment
    SPA->>IdP: Fetch userinfo
    SPA->>API: Log in with derived local password
    API-->>SPA: Session JWT
```

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): The client checks the redirect URI against a configured allow-list. It uses the implicit flow (response_type=token) with no state parameter or PKCE. The access token is put in a URL query string when calling the userinfo endpoint. -->
This is a frontend identity hint converted into a local login, not a server-side authorization-code flow:

- The local password is derived from the public email address, so anyone who knows the email can log in as that user (🔴 [F-004](#f-004)).
- The implicit flow sends no state, nonce or PKCE (🟡 [F-084](#f-084)).
- The userinfo response is trusted without checking the token audience (🟡 [F-083](#f-083)).



**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="password-change-transport"></a>
#### 6.2.2 Password change transport

**Status:** 🔴 Unsafe - current and new passwords travel in GET query strings.

`user.service.ts` concatenates the current, new and repeated password into a GET URL for the change-password route, which updates the stored hash.

The diagram shows the intended password-change path:
```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor User
    participant SPA as Angular Client
    participant API as Change Password Route
    participant DB as Users Table
    User->>SPA: Enter current and new password
    SPA->>API: GET change-password with query parameters
    API->>DB: Update password hash
    API-->>SPA: Confirmation
```

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): Current and new passwords are sent as GET query parameters. This exposes them to logs, history and referrers. -->
Two independent weaknesses sit on this path:

- Passwords are placed in the URL, which exposes them to server logs, browser history and referrers (🟡 [F-134](#f-134), 🟠 [F-052](#f-052)).
- The route does not verify the current password before changing it (🟠 [F-015](#f-015)).



**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="hardcoded-test-credentials-in-client"></a>
**Hardcoded test credentials in client.**


**Status:** 🔴 Unsafe - a testing account's credentials are shipped in the public client bundle.

`login.component.ts` defines a testing username and password, and tutorial hint code in the same bundle holds further literal account passwords.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): A testing username and password are embedded in the shipped client bundle. -->
Anyone who downloads the bundle can read the credentials:

- The testing account password is embedded in client code (🟡 [F-085](#f-085)).
- Tutorial resolvers compare input against literal account passwords (🟠 [F-058](#f-058)).
- The login handler also embeds seeded account passwords (🟡 [F-140](#f-140)).

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="two-factor-authentication-step-up-at-login"></a>
#### 6.2.3 Two-factor authentication step-up at login

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - the second factor works once enrolled, but SQL injection skips the first factor.

When a user has a TOTP secret, `routes/login.ts` returns a temporary token instead of a session token, and the client posts the one-time code to the 2FA verify route.

The diagram shows the positive login path with the branch into TOTP verification:
```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor User
    participant SPA as Angular Client
    participant API as Login and 2FA Routes
    User->>SPA: Email and password
    SPA->>API: POST login
    API-->>SPA: Temporary token when TOTP is set
    User->>SPA: One-time code
    SPA->>API: POST 2fa verify with temporary token
    API-->>SPA: Session JWT
```

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): When a TOTP secret is set, login returns a temporary token requiring a second factor. The SQL injection in the first-factor query can still bypass the password check. The /rest/2fa/verify handler was not inspected. -->
The factor is optional in effect and weakened around its edges:

- The first-factor query is injectable, so the password check can be bypassed (🔴 [F-010](#f-010)).
- TOTP secrets are stored in plaintext next to the password hash (🟡 [F-112](#f-112), 🟡 [F-138](#f-138)).
- The temporary 2FA token is kept in browser storage readable by scripts (🟠 [F-059](#f-059) — Session JWT and 2FA token stored in localStorage and script-readable cookie (Session JWT and 2FA token stored in `localStorage` and script-readable cookie)).
- The `/rest/2fa/verify` handler was not inspected.



**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="brute-force-and-credential-stuffing-protection-on-login"></a>
**Brute-force and credential-stuffing protection on login.**


**Status:** 🔴 Missing - the login handler has no lockout or throttling.

This control is not evidenced in the repository.

The diagram shows the login request path as it exists, with no throttling step between request and credential check:
```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor User
    participant API as Login Route
    participant DB as Users Table
    User->>API: POST login with email and password
    API->>DB: Look up user and password hash
    DB-->>API: User row
    API-->>User: Session JWT
```

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): The login handler has no lockout, throttling or rate limiting. Rate limiting could be applied elsewhere, for example in server.ts, which was not inspected. -->
Guessing attempts against login are not slowed in the inspected code:

- The login handler applies no lockout, throttling or rate limit, so credential stuffing runs at network speed.
- The only limiter covers reset-password and 2FA, and its key can be bypassed (🟡 [F-077](#f-077)).
- Rate limiting set elsewhere in `server.ts` was not inspected.



**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="user-registration"></a>
#### 6.2.4 User Registration

**Status:** 🔴 Unsafe - registration accepts a client-supplied admin role and imposes no password policy.

<!-- Model implementation note (facts for the intro): Present at POST /api/Users -->
Registration is `POST /api/Users`, served by a generated `finale` resource with a pre-processing check in `server.ts` and a model-level role validator.

The diagram shows the registration path:
```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor User
    participant API as Users Endpoint
    participant DB as Users Table
    User->>API: POST /api/Users with email and password
    API->>DB: Create user record
    DB-->>API: Stored user
    API-->>User: Created user
```

**Security assessment**

Registration lets a caller choose their own privilege:

- The request body can set the admin role because the role field is not stripped (🟠 [F-071](#f-071), 🟠 [F-072](#f-072)).
- No password strength policy applies (🟡 [F-079](#f-079)).
- The sensitive registration routes lack authentication middleware by design, which is acceptable here, but the same gap exists on other routes (🟠 [F-066](#f-066)).



**Relevant findings**

- 🟠 [F-066](#f-066) — Sensitive Routes Registered Without Authentication Middleware
- 🟠 [F-071](#f-071) — Self-registration accepts a client-supplied admin role
- 🟠 [F-072](#f-072) — Self-registration can set the admin role through the request
- 🟡 [F-079](#f-079) — No password strength policy on registration or change
- 🟡 [F-149](#f-149) — Unanchored regex on untrusted socket data

<a id="password-reset"></a>
#### 6.2.5 Password Reset

**Status:** 🔴 Unsafe - reset is authorized by a guessable security answer and returns the full user record.

<!-- Model implementation note (facts for the intro): Present at POST /rest/user/reset-password -->
`routes/resetPassword.ts` serves `POST /rest/user/reset-password`. It compares the submitted security answer with the stored HMAC and, on a match, writes the new password.

The diagram shows the reset path:
```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor User
    participant SPA as Angular Client
    participant API as Reset Route
    participant DB as Users and Answers
    User->>SPA: Email, security answer, new password
    SPA->>API: POST reset-password
    API->>DB: Compare hashed answer
    API->>DB: Update password
    API-->>SPA: Response
```

**Security assessment**

The reset path has four independent defects:

- A matching security answer alone authorizes the change, with no emailed token or second factor (🟠 [F-020](#f-020), 🟠 [F-021](#f-021)).
- The security question lookup is unauthenticated, which helps targeting (🟡 [F-120](#f-120)).
- No minimum password strength applies (🟡 [F-087](#f-087)).
- The response returns the full user record (🟡 [F-136](#f-136)), and the rate limit is bypassable (🟡 [F-077](#f-077)).



**Relevant findings**

- 🟠 [F-020](#f-020) — Password reset accepts a security-question answer
- 🟠 [F-021](#f-021) — Password reset relies on a guessable security
- 🟡 [F-077](#f-077) — Reset-password rate limit keyed on client-controlled IP
- 🟡 [F-087](#f-087) — No minimum password strength on reset
- 🟡 [F-120](#f-120) — Unauthenticated security question lookup enables
- 🟡 [F-136](#f-136) — Password reset response returns full user record

### 6.3 Session and Token Controls

<a id="ctrl-session-and-token-controls"></a>

**Verdict:** 🟠 Weak - one `RS256` token format is used for every session, but its key is public in source and the token is readable by scripts.

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.3.1 Client-side token storage](#631-client-side-token-storage)
- [6.3.2 JWT signing key management](#632-jwt-signing-key-management)
- [6.3.3 Session Token Issuance](#633-session-token-issuance)

**Implemented controls:** Client-side token storage, JWT signing key management, Session Token Issuance (JWT Based).

**Assessment:** This application uses a single locally-signed JWT for every session, regardless of the [§6.2](#62-identity-and-authentication-controls) flow that established it. The sub-sections trace the token through storage, key management and issuance. Verification does not pin the algorithm (🟠 [F-014](#f-014), 🟠 [F-019](#f-019), 🟠 [F-076](#f-076)), and the server-side token map ignores expiry and revocation (🟡 [F-088](#f-088)).

**Findings in this category without a control:**

- 🟠 [F-059](#f-059) — Session JWT and 2FA token stored in localStorage and script-readable cookie
- 🟡 [F-088](#f-088) — Session map lookup ignores token expiry and revocation
- 🟡 [F-141](#f-141) — Session token cookie set without HttpOnly or Secure

<a id="client-side-token-storage"></a>
#### 6.3.1 Client-side token storage

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - the token is readable by any script on the page.

<!-- Model implementation note (facts for the intro): frontend/src/app/login/login.component.ts:101-105,120 -->
After login, `login.component.ts` writes the JWT to `localStorage` and to a script-set cookie, and `request.interceptor.ts` reads it to build the `Authorization` header.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): The JWT is stored in localStorage and in a script-set cookie with no HttpOnly flag, so any XSS can read it. The cookie has an 8-hour expiry. The 2FA temporary token is also kept in localStorage. -->
Browser-side storage exposes the session to any XSS:

- The JWT and the 2FA temporary token sit in `localStorage` and a cookie without HttpOnly (🟠 [F-059](#f-059) — Session JWT and 2FA token stored in localStorage and script-readable cookie (Session JWT and 2FA token stored in `localStorage` and script-readable cookie)).
- The session cookie is also set without the Secure flag (🟡 [F-141](#f-141)).

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="jwt-signing-key-management"></a>
#### 6.3.2 JWT signing key management

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - the signing key is a literal in source, so anyone with the code can forge tokens.

`lib/insecurity.ts` holds the RSA private key used to sign session tokens, plus an HMAC secret, as string literals loaded with the application.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): The RSA private key used to sign JWTs is embedded as a literal in source code. The HMAC secret is also hardcoded. Anyone with source access can forge tokens. -->
Key material is committed rather than injected:

- The RSA signing key is hardcoded, so forged tokens for any user pass verification (🟠 [F-016](#f-016), 🟠 [F-022](#f-022)).
- The HMAC key for security answers and the cookie-signing secret are also hardcoded (🟡 [F-139](#f-139), 🟡 [F-128](#f-128)).

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="session-token-issuance"></a><a id="session-token-issuance-jwt-based"></a>
#### 6.3.3 Session Token Issuance

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - `RS256` with a 6 hour expiry is sound, but the key is hardcoded and there is no revocation.

`lib/insecurity.ts` signs a JWT with `RS256` and a 6 hour lifetime on login. The `isAuthorized` middleware verifies it with `express-jwt` and the public key, and a process-local map tracks issued tokens.

The diagram shows token issuance and later validation:
```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor User
    participant API as Login Route
    participant Auth as isAuthorized
    User->>API: Credentials
    API->>API: Sign JWT with RS256 and 6h expiry
    API-->>User: Session JWT
    User->>Auth: Request with Authorization header
    Auth->>Auth: Verify signature with public key
    Auth-->>User: Route handler runs
```

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): Tokens are RS256-signed with a 6h expiry, and the isAuthorized middleware verifies them with express-jwt using the public key. The weaknesses are that the signing key is hardcoded and that the token store is an in-memory map with no revocation or logout invalidation seen in the inspected code. -->
Issuance is reasonable, but the surrounding lifecycle has gaps:

- Verification passes no algorithm allow-list (🟠 [F-014](#f-014), 🟠 [F-019](#f-019), 🟠 [F-076](#f-076)).
- Issuer and audience are not checked (🟡 [F-086](#f-086)).
- The token map lookup ignores expiry and revocation, and no logout invalidation was seen (🟡 [F-088](#f-088)).
- The issued token embeds the password hash and TOTP secret (🟡 [F-135](#f-135)).



**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

### 6.4 Authorization Controls

<a id="ctrl-authorization-controls"></a>


**Systemic weaknesses:** [W-005](#w-005), [W-006](#w-006), [W-022](#w-022), [W-023](#w-023), [W-024](#w-024), [W-025](#w-025)
**Verdict:** 🟠 Weak - guards cover baskets and the B2B API, but object ownership is missing on most data routes.

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.4.1 Route-level authorization middleware](#641-route-level-authorization-middleware)
- [6.4.2 LLM chatbot tool authorization and parameter binding](#642-llm-chatbot-tool-authorization-and-parameter-binding)
- [6.4.3 Wallet ownership verification for NFT mint and wallet endpoints](#643-wallet-ownership-verification-for-nft-mint-and-wallet-endpoints)
- [6.4.4 Ownership-scoped review update](#644-ownership-scoped-review-update)
- [6.4.5 Server-side token session map and user header trust](#645-server-side-token-session-map-and-user-header-trust)

**Implemented controls:** Route-level authorization middleware, LLM chatbot tool authorization and parameter binding, Wallet ownership verification for NFT mint and wallet endpoints, Ownership-scoped review update (author match), Server-side token session map and user header trust.

**Assessment:** Route middleware is applied unevenly and object ownership is rarely checked: ownership gaps on baskets, orders, reviews and generated resources (🔴 [F-001](#f-001), 🟠 [F-002](#f-002), 🟠 [F-003](#f-003)), unguarded product writes (🟠 [F-068](#f-068), 🟠 [F-069](#f-069)), and client-chosen owner fields (🟡 [F-169](#f-169)). The chatbot and wallet surfaces add tool and ownership gaps.

**Findings in this category without a control:**

- 🔴 [F-001](#f-001) — Attacker-Controlled Owner ID Authorization Bypass

- 🟠 [F-063](#f-063) — Basket item creation bypasses basket ownership
- 🟠 [F-064](#f-064) — Any authenticated user can rewrite other
- 🟠 [F-065](#f-065) — Server access logs downloadable without authentication or role
- 🟠 [F-066](#f-066) — Sensitive Routes Registered Without Authentication Middleware
- 🟠 [F-068](#f-068) — Unauthenticated product modification
- 🟠 [F-069](#f-069) — Missing authorization on generated product write endpoints
- 🟠 [F-070](#f-070) — Chatbot coupon tool mints any discount
- 🟠 [F-071](#f-071) — Self-registration accepts a client-supplied admin role
- 🟠 [F-072](#f-072) — Self-registration can set the admin role through the request
- 🟡 [F-100](#f-100) — Basket item update accepts client-supplied BasketId
- 🟡 [F-101](#f-101) — Feedback can be created in another user's name
- 🟡 [F-111](#f-111) — Client-side coupon validation trusts browser-supplied

<a id="route-level-authorization-middleware"></a>
#### 6.4.1 Route-level authorization middleware

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - guards exist on core routes, but coverage is inconsistent and ownership is not checked.

`isAuthorized` and `denyAll` guard baskets, basket items, complaints, privacy requests and the B2B API, and `isAccounting` guards the order-history admin routes.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): isAuthorized and denyAll are applied to baskets, basket items, complaints, privacy requests, and the B2B API. isAccounting guards the order-history admin routes. Coverage is inconsistent: the PUT /api/Products isAuthorized line is commented out. Several routes have no visible guard (/rest/chat, /metrics, /rest/admin/application-configuration, and /api/Addresss/:id PUT only appends the user ID). The /api/Quantitys isAccounting route is paired with an IP filter that uses a malformed allow-list. -->
Coverage depends on each route author remembering a guard:

- Product writes are unguarded because the `isAuthorized` line is commented out (🟠 [F-068](#f-068), 🟠 [F-069](#f-069)).
- Several sensitive routes register with no middleware (🟠 [F-066](#f-066)), including log files (🟠 [F-065](#f-065)) and the metrics endpoint (🟡 [F-119](#f-119)).
- Guarded routes check only JWT validity, so any signed-in user lists all accounts (🟡 [F-172](#f-172)).

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="llm-chatbot-tool-authorization-and-parameter-binding"></a>
#### 6.4.2 LLM chatbot tool authorization and parameter binding

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - the coupon limit lives only in the prompt, and tool calls are not bound to server-side policy.

`routes/chat.ts` defines the model's tools: product search, order lookup, product reviews and coupon generation. The order lookup compares the caller server-side, and product search uses bound parameters.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): Inspected scope covers the tool definitions in chat.ts. The coupon-policy limit (max 10%) exists only in the system prompt and the tool description. The server-side schema accepts any number, and execute generates a coupon for any discount value. getOrderById enforces ownership server-side (a masked-email comparison). getProductReviews builds a \$where string from a numeric conversion of the model-supplied id. The searchProducts query uses Sequelize parameter binding. The system prompt embeds a confidential discount instruction and the user name. Approval flows, memory writes and MCP are not present in the inspected slice. -->
The model's output is trusted more than it should be:

- The 10 percent coupon cap appears only in the prompt, so the tool mints any discount (🟠 [F-070](#f-070), 🟠 [F-073](#f-073), 🟠 [F-075](#f-075)).
- Client-supplied history and roles reach the model (🟠 [F-036](#f-036)).
- The system prompt carries a confidential discount rule that can be extracted (🟡 [F-122](#f-122)).
- Approval flows, memory writes and MCP are not present in the inspected slice.

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="wallet-ownership-verification-for-nft-mint-and-wallet-endpoints"></a>
#### 6.4.3 Wallet ownership verification for NFT mint and wallet endpoints

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - wallet ownership is taken from the request body, with no proof of control.

`routes/nftMint.ts` and `routes/web3Wallet.ts` read `walletAddress` from the body, and `walletNFTVerify` checks membership in an in-memory set filled from on-chain `NFTMinted` events.

The diagram shows the wallet claim path:
```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Caller
    participant API as Wallet Handlers
    participant Set as Minted Address Set
    Caller->>API: POST walletAddress
    API->>Set: Check membership
    Set-->>API: Found or not found
    API-->>Caller: Challenge result
```

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): The wallet address comes from the request body (req.body.walletAddress). The inspected code has no signature or ownership proof. Any caller can claim any address. walletNFTVerify checks only membership in an in-memory set filled from on-chain NFTMinted events. Within the inspected files, no authentication on these handlers was found. Route-level middleware in server.ts was not inspected, so it is unknown. The component is a challenge/training surface, so the impact is limited. -->
Any caller can claim any address:

- No signature or ownership proof ties the caller to the wallet (🟡 [F-081](#f-081)).
- Authentication on these handlers was not found in the inspected files, and route-level middleware in `server.ts` was not inspected, so it is unknown.
- This is a challenge and training surface, so impact is limited.



**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="ownership-scoped-review-update"></a><a id="ownership-scoped-review-update-author-match"></a>
#### 6.4.4 Ownership-scoped review update

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - the reference fix binds the update to the author, but still accepts operator objects.

The reference fix for review updates scopes the selector to the authenticated user's email as author. It is a code-fix snippet, not the live route.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): The reference fix scopes the update selector to the authenticated user's email as author, which prevents forging or editing other users' reviews. It does not validate the type of req.body.id, so operator injection remains possible. It also sets multi:true. This is a snippet, not the live route. -->
The snippet closes one hole and leaves others:

- It does not validate the type of the review id, so operator injection remains possible (🟡 [F-102](#f-102)).
- It sets `multi: true`, so one request can touch many reviews.
- The live route lets any signed-in user rewrite other users' reviews (🟠 [F-064](#f-064)).

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="server-side-token-session-map-and-user-header-trust"></a>
#### 6.4.5 Server-side token session map and user header trust

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - identity comes from a process-local map and a client-supplied header.

`authenticatedUsers` is an in-memory map keyed by token, and `userEmailFrom` reads the caller's identity from the `x-user-email` header.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): authenticatedUsers is a process-local in-memory map keyed by token. userEmailFrom reads identity from the client-supplied x-user-email header, which is not a trustworthy identity source. -->
Neither source is trustworthy:

- The `x-user-email` header is chosen by the client, so it cannot establish who the caller is.
- Review authorship is likewise taken from the request (🟡 [F-078](#f-078), 🟡 [F-154](#f-154)).
- The map lives in one process and ignores expiry and revocation (🟡 [F-088](#f-088)).

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

### 6.5 Query Construction and Data Access Controls

<a id="ctrl-query-construction-and-data-access-controls"></a>


**Systemic weaknesses:** [W-002](#w-002)
**Verdict:** 🔴 Unsafe - the login query is raw SQL and several NoSQL selectors accept request-controlled structure.

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.5.1 NoSQL query operator-injection guard on review update](#651-nosql-query-operator-injection-guard-on-review-update)

- [6.5.3 Login query construction](#653-login-query-construction)

**Assessment:** Sequelize is bypassed on the login and search routes, which build raw SQL (🔴 [F-010](#f-010), 🔴 [F-005](#f-005)), and MarsDB selectors take request input in executable predicates (🟠 [F-031](#f-031) (NoSQL \$where injection), 🟠 [F-032](#f-032) — Request input in executable MongoDB predicates, 🟠 [F-033](#f-033) (NoSQL \$where injection in order tracking)).

**Findings in this category without a control:**

- 🔴 [F-005](#f-005) — SQL injection in product search query
- 🔴 [F-010](#f-010) — SQL injection in login query through the email field
- 🟠 [F-031](#f-031) — NoSQL \$where injection
- 🟠 [F-032](#f-032) — Request input in executable MongoDB predicates
- 🟠 [F-033](#f-033) — NoSQL \$where injection in order tracking
- 🟡 [F-102](#f-102) — Review update lacks ownership check

<a id="nosql-query-operator-injection-guard-on-review-update"></a><a id="nosql-query-operator-injection-guard-on-review-update-reference-fix"></a>
#### 6.5.1 NoSQL query operator-injection guard on review update

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - the guard rejects operator objects but does not bind the update to the author.

The reference fix rejects non-string review ids with a 400 before calling `reviewsCollection.update`. It is a static snippet, not the live route.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): The reference fix rejects non-string review ids with a 400 before calling reviewsCollection.update, which blocks operator objects in the _id selector. It does not bind the update to the authenticated author, and the user value is read but unused. This is a static code-fix snippet, not the live route, so it shows intended hardening only. -->
The snippet shows intended hardening only:

- It does not bind the update to the authenticated author, and the user value is read but unused (🟡 [F-102](#f-102)).
- The live route still accepts request-controlled structure (🟠 [F-032](#f-032)).

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="marsdb-embedded-collection-access-boundary"></a>
#### 6.5.2 MarsDB embedded collection access boundary

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - collections are plain in-process objects with no access wrapper.

The reviews and orders collections are exported as plain in-process MarsDB collections and used directly by route handlers.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): The reviews and orders collections are exported as plain in-process MarsDB collections. Within this file there is no schema validation, access wrapper or per-collection authorization, so every caller gets unrestricted read and write access. Enforcement depends on each calling route. -->
Every caller gets unrestricted read and write access:

- No schema validation, access wrapper or per-collection authorization exists in this file, so enforcement depends on each route.
- Routes pass request input into `$where` predicates (🟠 [F-031](#f-031) — NoSQL \$where injection (NoSQL \$where injection), 🟠 [F-033](#f-033) — NoSQL \$where injection in order tracking (NoSQL \$where injection in order tracking)).

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="login-query-construction"></a>
#### 6.5.3 Login query construction

**Status:** 🔴 Unsafe - the email is concatenated into raw SQL, so login can be bypassed.

`routes/login.ts` looks the user up with `models.sequelize.query()`, using a template literal that includes `req.body.email` and the password hash.

The diagram shows the login lookup:
```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor User
    participant API as Login Route
    participant DB as SQLite
    User->>API: Email and password
    API->>DB: Raw SQL with email inserted
    DB-->>API: Matching user row
    API-->>User: Session JWT
```

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): The login query concatenates req.body.email directly into a raw SQL string via a template literal, with no parameter binding. The email field is attacker-controlled, so authentication can be bypassed by SQL injection. -->
The query has no parameter binding:

- A crafted email value changes the query structure and returns an arbitrary user, so authentication is bypassed (🔴 [F-010](#f-010)).
- Product search uses the same raw construction (🔴 [F-005](#f-005)).



**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="ctrl-input-boundary-validation-controls"></a>

**Verdict:** 🔴 Missing - validation is ad hoc per route, with no schema layer and unbounded inputs in several handlers.

- 🟡 [F-143](#f-143) — Unbounded LLM consumption on unauthenticated chat endpoint
- 🟡 [F-144](#f-144) — Unbounded unauthenticated LLM consumption on chat endpoint
- 🟡 [F-145](#f-145) — Unbounded in-memory code-fix cache keyed by request

<a id="validation-approach"></a>
#### 6.6.1 Validation Approach

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - checks are written per handler, with no shared request schema.

Sequelize model validators cover some fields, and individual routes add their own checks. No central request schema layer was evidenced.

**Security assessment**

Business-rule limits are inconsistent:

- Wallet top-up credits a client-chosen amount (🟠 [F-034](#f-034)).
- Basket item quantity has no range validation (🟡 [F-090](#f-090)).
- The coupon cap is enforced only in prompt text (🟠 [F-073](#f-073)).

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="upload-size-and-type-validation"></a>
#### 6.6.2 Upload size and type validation

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - a type allow-list and sanitized names exist, but the type is client-declared.

The memory upload uses a mimetype allow-list and sanitized filenames, then writes files to disk. A 200 KB memory-storage size limit applies to other uploads.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): Memory upload uses a mimetype allow-list and sanitized filenames. Memory uploads are written to disk. The 200 KB memory-storage size limit applies to other uploads. The mimetype is client-declared, so it is not a content check. -->
The limits leave gaps:

- The mimetype is client-declared, so it is not a content check.
- The memory image upload is unbounded and written before authentication (🟡 [F-147](#f-147)).
- Fetched profile images are stored without type validation (🟡 [F-098](#f-098)).

<a id="request-input-validation-for-walletaddress"></a>
#### 6.6.3 Request input validation for walletAddress

**Status:** 🔴 Missing - `walletAddress` is used without any type or format check.

This control is not evidenced in the repository.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): walletAddress is used unvalidated. contractExploitListener adds it to an unbounded in-memory Set on every call, before any check. Repeated calls can grow memory without limit. There is no type or format check, and no rate limiting was seen in these files. -->
The address is used as received:

- `contractExploitListener` adds it to an unbounded in-memory Set on every call, before any check (🟡 [F-146](#f-146)).
- No rate limiting was seen in these files, so repeated calls can grow memory without limit.

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="socket-message-handler-input-validation"></a>
#### 6.6.4 Socket message handler input validation

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - handlers take untyped data, with a few targeted checks.

`registerWebsocketEvents.ts` registers handlers for notifications, SVG redirect checks and closing notifications. The SVG handler uses a regex plus `isRedirectAllowed`, and the close handler checks `Array.isArray`.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): Handlers take untyped data (any) with no schema validation. 'notification received' lets any client remove shared notifications by flag value; the SVG handler applies regex plus isRedirectAllowed, and the close-notifications handler checks Array.isArray. Messages are also not rate limited. -->
Most handlers trust their payload:

- Data is typed `any` with no schema, and any client can remove shared notifications by flag value.
- An unanchored regex runs on unbounded socket data (🟡 [F-149](#f-149)).
- Messages are not rate limited.

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

#### 6.6.5 Client-side input handling for guest basket and URL encoding

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - basket and order ID handling is partly validated, but only on the client.

<!-- Model implementation note (facts for the intro): basket.service.ts:82-108; track-order.service.ts:21 -->
`basket.service.ts` validates guest basket items as integers and bounds them on read-back, and `track-order.service.ts` URI-encodes the order ID.

**Security assessment**

<a id="output-encoding-and-client-side-rendering"></a>
#### 6.7.1 Output Encoding and Client-Side Rendering

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - default escaping exists but trusted-HTML calls and unsanitized storage defeat it.

Pages that show user-provided text escape it by default. Feedback, product, user and tracking views render stored or request-derived text as HTML instead.

**Security assessment**

Most sinks mark data as trusted HTML before rendering through `bypassSecurityTrustHtml()` and `[innerHTML]`. The sources are feedback comments, user emails, product descriptions, challenge text, the last login IP and tracking results (🟠 [F-037](#f-037), 🟠 [F-038](#f-038), 🟠 [F-039](#f-039), 🟠 [F-040](#f-040), 🟡 [F-104](#f-104), 🟡 [F-105](#f-105), 🟡 [F-106](#f-106), 🟡 [F-107](#f-107), 🟡 [F-108](#f-108), 🟡 [F-109](#f-109)). Storage-side sanitization is skipped or weak for product descriptions and usernames (🟠 [F-023](#f-023), 🟡 [F-089](#f-089)). Search and tracking pages add DOM and reflected variants (🟠 [F-043](#f-043), 🟠 [F-044](#f-044), 🟠 [F-045](#f-045)).

**Relevant findings**

- 🟠 [F-023](#f-023) — Product description stored without HTML sanitization
- 🟠 [F-037](#f-037) — Stored XSS
- 🟠 [F-038](#f-038) — Stored XSS
- 🟠 [F-039](#f-039) — Stored XSS
- 🟠 [F-040](#f-040) — Stored XSS

The `Socket.IO` server restricts CORS to a single hardcoded origin, `http://localhost:4200`.

The restriction covers only part of the exposure:

**Verdict:** 🟠 Weak - the database is unencrypted, passwords use unsalted `MD5`, and signing and HMAC keys are in source.

**Implemented controls:** Database connection authentication and storage protection, Secret handling for the Alchemy API key and WSS transport, Database storage protection and access configuration, Password hashing.

**Assessment:** Secrets and sensitive data are unprotected: hardcoded RSA, HMAC and cookie secrets (🟠 [F-016](#f-016), 🟠 [F-022](#f-022), 🟡 [F-128](#f-128), 🟡 [F-139](#f-139)), card numbers and TOTP secrets in cleartext (🟠 [F-046](#f-046), 🟡 [F-112](#f-112)), and an unencrypted SQLite file (🟡 [F-133](#f-133)).
