<a id="database-connection-authentication-and-storage-protection"></a>
#### 6.9.1 Database connection authentication and storage protection

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - the database engine has no authentication and no encryption at rest.

Sequelize opens an embedded SQLite file at `data/juiceshop.sqlite` using placeholder credentials.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): Sequelize uses an embedded SQLite file (data/juiceshop.sqlite) with placeholder credentials; the engine has no authentication, and no encryption at rest is configured in this file. Protection depends on filesystem and process isolation, which were not inspected here. -->
Protection depends entirely on the host:

- The file is unencrypted, with no SQLCipher or key option (🟡 [F-133](#f-133)).
- Payment card numbers are stored in cleartext in it (🟠 [F-046](#f-046)).
- Filesystem and process isolation were not inspected.

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="secret-handling-for-the-alchemy-api-key-and-wss-transport"></a>
#### 6.9.2 Secret handling for the Alchemy API key and WSS transport

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - the key comes from the environment over TLS, but a missing value fails silently.

The Alchemy API key is read from an environment variable and used in a `wss` URL for the RPC provider. Contract addresses are public constants.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): The API key is read from the environment, not hardcoded, and the RPC connection uses TLS (wss). If the variable is unset, the code falls back to an empty string, so the connection silently fails and is not rejected at startup. The key is embedded in the URL path. The contract addresses are public constants. -->
The handling is better than elsewhere in the codebase:

- An unset variable falls back to an empty string, so the connection fails silently and is not rejected at startup.
- The key sits in the URL path.
- Other secrets are hardcoded, including a wallet mnemonic (🟡 [F-131](#f-131)).

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="database-storage-protection-and-access-configuration"></a>
#### 6.9.3 Database storage protection and access configuration

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - the SQLite file is unencrypted and its credentials are not enforced.

Sequelize is configured with the placeholder credentials `username` and `password` against a local SQLite file.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): Sequelize is configured with an unencrypted SQLite file at data/juiceshop.sqlite. The engine has no authentication of its own, and the placeholder credentials 'username'/'password' are not enforced. Protection relies on process and filesystem boundaries, which were not inspected here. No encryption at rest was observed in this file. -->
Access relies on boundaries that were not examined:

- The engine has no authentication of its own, so the placeholder credentials are not enforced.
- No encryption at rest was observed (🟡 [F-133](#f-133)).
- Sensitive columns are stored in cleartext (🟠 [F-046](#f-046), 🟡 [F-112](#f-112)).

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="password-hashing"></a><a id="password-hashing-and-credential-storage"></a>
#### 6.9.4 Password Hashing and Credential Storage

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - passwords are hashed with unsalted `MD5`.

The `password` setter in `models/user.ts` stores `security.hash()`, and the same helper compares login input. A separate HMAC helper hashes security answers.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): Passwords are hashed with unsalted MD5, which is fast and vulnerable to rainbow-table and brute-force attacks. The HMAC helper uses a hardcoded key in source. -->
The hash choice makes a database dump directly useful:

- Passwords use a fast unsalted `MD5` digest with no work factor (🟠 [F-047](#f-047) — Passwords stored and compared as unsalted MD5 (Passwords stored and compared as unsalted `MD5`), 🟠 [F-056](#f-056) — Passwords hashed and compared as unsalted MD5 (Passwords hashed and compared as unsalted `MD5`)).
- Security answers use HMAC-SHA256 under a hardcoded key (🟡 [F-113](#f-113), 🟡 [F-139](#f-139)).

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="outbound-url-fetch-restrictions"></a><a id="outbound-url-fetch-restrictions-profile-image-url-webhook-llm"></a>
#### 6.10.1 Outbound URL fetch restrictions

**Status:** 🔴 Missing - the profile image fetch accepts any requester-supplied URL.

This control is not evidenced in the repository.

The diagram shows the profile image URL flow as it runs today:
```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor User
    participant API as Profile Image Route
    participant Remote as Remote Host
    participant Disk as Server Disk
    User->>API: POST imageUrl
    API->>Remote: fetch imageUrl
    Remote-->>API: Response body
    API->>Disk: Write file
    API-->>User: Updated profile image
```

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): The profile image handler passes the requester-supplied imageUrl directly to fetch with no scheme, host or private-address allow-list, so server-side request forgery is possible. The fetched body is written to disk. The code even flags a match on a server-side challenge URL. The webhook and LLM model-check calls use configured, not requester-supplied, URLs. The model check has a 5s timeout. -->
Outbound requests are not restricted:

- `imageUrl` goes straight to `fetch` with no scheme, host or private-address allow-list, so server-side request forgery is possible (🟠 [F-057](#f-057)).
- The fetched body is written to disk without type validation (🟡 [F-098](#f-098)).
- The webhook and LLM model-check calls use configured URLs, and the model check has a 5 second timeout.



**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

### 6.11 Operations Runtime and Supply Chain Controls

**Build path.** [Figure 1b](#figure-1b) shows the inputs, build systems and release artifacts that Juice Shop evidences.

Highlighted path of 🟠 [F-025](#f-025) — Lockfile generation disabled by npm configuration — .npmrc:1: npm registry → GitHub Actions → docker.io/bkimminich/juice-shop. The path stops where the evidence ends.

**Attack entries:**

- **Manipulated dependency**
  - 🟠 [F-025](#f-025) — Lockfile generation disabled by npm configuration
  - 🟡 [F-093](#f-093) — Untrusted npm Install/Postinstall Scripts Enabled
  - 🟡 [F-095](#f-095) — Release job installs global build tools and dependencies
- **Manipulated CI input**
  - 🟠 [F-024](#f-024) — GitHub Actions referenced by mutable tags or branches
  - 🟡 [F-092](#f-092) — Heroku CLI installed by piping unverified remote script to shell
  - 🟡 [F-094](#f-094) — Container base images referenced by floating tags without digest
- **Replaced release artifact**
  - 🟡 [F-091](#f-091) — Published container images lack signature and provenance attestation

**Not attributable to a build step:**

- 🟡 [F-151](#f-151) — Container Runs as Root


**Systemic weaknesses:** [W-026](#w-026), [W-042](#w-042)
**Verdict:** 🔴 Missing - the build pipeline lacks lockfile and install integrity, and request throttling is thin.

**Controls covered:**

- [6.11.1 Database concurrency and transaction handling](#6111-database-concurrency-and-transaction-handling)

**Implemented controls:** Database concurrency and transaction handling, GitHub Actions pinning to commit SHAs, Workflow token permissions and secret scoping, Release and container image build hardening, Static analysis and dependency install hygiene in CI.

**Assessment:** CI hardening is mixed: SHA pinning and a non-root distroless image help, but actions use mutable tags (🟠 [F-024](#f-024)), lockfiles are disabled (🟠 [F-025](#f-025)), install scripts run (🟡 [F-093](#f-093)), and images are unsigned (🟡 [F-091](#f-091)). Rate limiting covers only reset and 2FA.

<a id="database-concurrency-and-transaction-handling"></a>
#### 6.11.1 Database concurrency and transaction handling

**Status:** 🟢 Adequate - transactions and retries give sound consistency for the embedded database.

The Sequelize SQLite setup uses IMMEDIATE transactions and retries up to 5 times on `SQLITE_BUSY`.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): Retries on SQLITE_BUSY (max 5) and IMMEDIATE transactions give reasonable availability and consistency handling for the embedded database. Query logging is disabled (logging: false), so no database-level audit trail is produced here. -->
The handling suits the embedded database:

- Retries and IMMEDIATE mode keep concurrent writes consistent.
- Query logging is disabled (`logging: false`), so no database-level audit trail exists.

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="github-actions-pinning-to-commit-shas"></a>
#### 6.11.2 GitHub Actions pinning to commit SHAs

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - most actions are SHA-pinned, but a few use mutable tags.

<!-- Model implementation note (facts for the intro): ci.yml, release.yml and codeql-analysis.yml uses: lines -->
`ci.yml`, `release.yml` and `codeql-analysis.yml` reference most third-party actions by full commit SHA.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): Most third-party actions are pinned to full commit SHAs. coverallsapp/github-action@v2 and github/codeql-action@v3 use mutable tags. The coveralls step receives GITHUB_TOKEN. release.yml uses an archived action (dawidd6/action-get-tag), as its own TODO notes. -->
The pinning is incomplete:

- `coverallsapp/github-action@v2` and `github/codeql-action@v3` use mutable tags, and the coveralls step receives `GITHUB_TOKEN` (🟠 [F-024](#f-024)).
- `release.yml` uses an archived action, as its own TODO notes.

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="workflow-token-permissions-and-secret-scoping"></a>
#### 6.11.3 Workflow token permissions and secret scoping

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - only the CodeQL workflow declares least-privilege permissions.

<!-- Model implementation note (facts for the intro): permissions block in codeql-analysis.yml only; secrets context usage elsewhere -->
`codeql-analysis.yml` has a `permissions` block. Other workflows pass secrets through the `secrets` context.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): The CodeQL workflow declares least-privilege permissions. The inspected ci.yml and release.yml have no permissions block, so GITHUB_TOKEN uses the repository default. Secrets are passed through the secrets context. ALCHEMY_API_KEY is scoped to a single test step, and the DockerHub credentials are used only in the release docker job. Other workflows were not inspected. -->
Token scope is inconsistent:

- `ci.yml` and `release.yml` have no permissions block, so `GITHUB_TOKEN` takes the repository default (🟡 [F-150](#f-150) — Workflows rely on default GITHUB_TOKEN permissions (Workflows rely on default `GITHUB_TOKEN` permissions), 🟡 [F-152](#f-152) — Missing Workflow Permissions Block).
- `ALCHEMY_API_KEY` is scoped to one test step and the DockerHub credentials only to the release docker job.
- Other workflows were not inspected.

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="release-and-container-image-build-hardening"></a>
#### 6.11.4 Release and container image build hardening

**Status:** 🟢 Adequate - a multi-stage distroless image runs as a non-root user and ships an SBOM.

<!-- Model implementation note (facts for the intro): Dockerfile multi-stage build, USER 65532, npm run sbom -->
The `Dockerfile` builds in stages, runs on a distroless runtime image as `USER 65532`, and `npm run sbom` generates an SBOM.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): The Dockerfile uses a multi-stage build with a distroless runtime image, runs as non-root user 65532, and generates an SBOM. The base images use floating tags, not digests. The release images are not signed and have no provenance attestation in the inspected files. -->
Supply-chain assurance stops short of verification:

- Base images use floating tags, not digests (🟡 [F-094](#f-094)).
- Release images carry no signature or provenance attestation (🟡 [F-091](#f-091)).
- 🟡 [F-151](#f-151) — Container Runs as Root reports a root container, which conflicts with the `USER 65532` evidence and needs reconciling.

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="static-analysis-and-dependency-install-hygiene-in-ci"></a>
#### 6.11.5 Static analysis and dependency install hygiene in CI

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - CodeQL runs on push and pull requests, but installs are not reproducible.

<!-- Model implementation note (facts for the intro): codeql-analysis.yml queries; npm install steps in ci.yml and release.yml -->
`codeql-analysis.yml` runs the security-extended query suite, and the lint job installs with `--ignore-scripts`.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): CodeQL runs the security-extended query suite on push and pull requests, and the lint job installs with —ignore-scripts. Test and release jobs use npm install, not npm ci, with install scripts enabled. Release tooling is installed globally with version ranges, so builds are not fully reproducible. -->
Install hygiene is uneven:

- Test and release jobs use `npm install` with install scripts enabled (🟡 [F-093](#f-093)).
- `.npmrc` disables lockfile generation (🟠 [F-025](#f-025)).
- Release tooling is installed globally with version ranges (🟡 [F-095](#f-095)), and the Heroku CLI is installed from a piped remote script (🟡 [F-092](#f-092)).

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="rate-limiting-and-access-logging"></a>
#### 6.11.6 Rate limiting and access logging

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - limits cover only reset-password and 2FA, and the reset key is spoofable.

`server.ts` applies `express-rate-limit` to reset-password and the 2FA endpoints. Morgan access logs rotate daily and keep two days.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): Rate limiting covers only reset-password and the 2FA endpoints. The reset-password keyGenerator trusts a client-controllable X-Forwarded-For header and 'trust proxy' is enabled, so it can be bypassed. Login and chat are not rate limited in the inspected lines. Morgan access logs rotate daily and keep two days. -->
Throttling misses the highest-value routes:

- The reset-password key trusts the client-controllable `X-Forwarded-For` header with `trust proxy` enabled (🟡 [F-077](#f-077)).
- Login and chat are not rate limited in the inspected lines, and unbounded chat use is a finding (🟡 [F-143](#f-143), 🟡 [F-144](#f-144)).
- Two days of access logs is a short retention window.

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="error-handling-and-logging-for-websocket-provider-failures"></a>
#### 6.11.7 Error handling and logging for WebSocket provider failures

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - errors are logged and the listener resets, but handling is thin.

The wallet event listener's `onerror` handler logs the error and resets the creation flag so the listener can be rebuilt.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): The onerror handler logs the error and resets the listener flag so it can be recreated. No provider cleanup or reconnect logic was seen. The creation flag is not guarded against concurrent requests, so duplicate listeners are possible. The catch blocks return utils.getErrorMessage(error) to the client. Whether that leaks detail was not inspected. -->
Failure handling is partial:

- No provider cleanup or reconnect logic was seen.
- The creation flag is not guarded against concurrent requests, so duplicate listeners are possible.
- Catch blocks return `utils.getErrorMessage(error)` to the client, and whether it leaks detail was not inspected.

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

#### 6.11.8 Database concurrency and transaction handling

**Status:** 🟢 Adequate - retries and IMMEDIATE transactions support consistent writes.

Transactions in the SQLite configuration use IMMEDIATE mode, and queries retry up to 5 times on `SQLITE_BUSY`.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): Transactions use IMMEDIATE mode, and queries retry up to 5 times on SQLITE_BUSY. This supports availability and integrity under concurrent writes. -->
The control supports availability and integrity under concurrent writes. It adds no audit trail, since query logging is disabled.

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

### 6.12 Real-time and Not Applicable Controls

**Verdict:** 🟠 Weak - the real-time channel is unauthenticated and the LLM tools are not bound to server-side policy.

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.12.1 Real-Time Channel Security](#6121-real-time-channel-security)
- [6.12.2 LLM Integration Security](#6122-llm-integration-security)

**Implemented controls:** Single-origin CORS on the `Socket.IO` server, a regex plus redirect allow-list in the SVG handler, an `Array.isArray` check on close-notifications, and server-side order ownership in the chatbot order tool.

**Assessment:** `Socket.IO` is present and the chatbot is an LLM integration, so neither domain is absent. Both accept untrusted input from unauthenticated callers (🟡 [F-132](#f-132), 🟠 [F-036](#f-036)).

<a id="real-time-channel-security"></a>
#### 6.12.1 Real-Time Channel Security

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - the channel limits one browser origin but accepts any client.

`registerWebsocketEvents.ts` runs a `Socket.IO` server that replays stored notifications on connect and handles notification, SVG and close events.

**Security assessment**

The channel has no caller identity:

- Any client receives every stored notification, including challenge flags (🟡 [F-132](#f-132)).
- Handler input is untyped, and an unanchored regex runs on it (🟡 [F-149](#f-149)).

**Relevant findings**

- None.

<a id="llm-integration-security"></a>
#### 6.12.2 LLM Integration Security

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - the model's tools are not constrained by server-side policy.

`routes/chat.ts` sends client messages and a system prompt to the model, which can call tools for product search, order lookup, reviews and coupon generation.

**Security assessment**

Prompt text stands in for policy:

- Client-supplied history and roles reach the model (🟠 [F-036](#f-036)), and stored usernames and reviews enter its context (🟡 [F-103](#f-103)).
- The coupon tool mints any discount (🟠 [F-070](#f-070), 🟠 [F-075](#f-075)), and the cap exists only in the prompt (🟠 [F-073](#f-073)).
- A confidential rule is extractable (🟡 [F-122](#f-122)).
- The unauthenticated route has no usage bound (🟡 [F-143](#f-143), 🟡 [F-144](#f-144)).

**Relevant findings**

- None.

### 6.13 Defense-in-Depth Summary

<!-- §6.13 FORMAT — NEVER a table, at most 120 words, no file:line. Two short bullet lists: **What holds** (the individual positive controls that exist, strongest first, e.g. distroless runtime image, RS256 algorithm choice) and **Repair first** (the control-boundary repairs that would restore layered defense, each with its mitigation link [M-NNN](#m-nnn)). Do NOT emit a Markdown table — `| header |` lines under §6.13 are a contract violation. -->

**What holds**

- Distroless, non-root runtime image with an SBOM.
- `RS256` as the token algorithm.
- Most CI actions pinned to commit SHAs, plus CodeQL.
- TOTP step-up for enrolled users.
- Bound parameters in the chatbot product search.

**Repair first**

- Parameterize login and search queries.
- Move signing keys and secrets out of source.
- Add server-side ownership and role checks on data routes.
- Strip role and owner fields from request bodies.
- Restrict outbound fetches and require socket authentication.

- 🟡 Medium · [W-041](#w-041) - Security-sensitive data uses weak cryptographic primitives · observed-practice · 3 findings · 2 components
- 🟡 Medium · [W-042](#w-042) - Build pipeline trusts mutable third-party references · design-risk · 4 findings · 1 component

<a id="w-001"></a>
### W-001 — Application data crosses into executable code

🔴 Critical · implementation weakness · confirmed · 2 findings

An interpreter evaluates application values as code. The affected path mixes data processing with executable program construction instead of constraining callers to predefined operations.

**Confirmed findings:**

- 🔴 [F-011](#f-011) — Server-side code execution

**Practice sites:**

- 🟠 [F-062](#f-062) — B2B order lines evaluated by JavaScript interpreter

**Affected components:** [C-03](#c-03)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — replace evaluation of application values with a data parser or a fixed set of operations whose arguments remain data
- **Tactical** — ● [M-187](#m-187) — Remove server-side evaluation of untrusted input (F-011), ◕ [M-229](#m-229) — Remove server-side evaluation of untrusted input (F-062)

<a id="w-002"></a>
### W-002 — Database access relies on concatenated queries

🔴 Critical · design weakness · confirmed · 2 findings

Database queries are assembled from application values instead of passing those values through an enforced parameterised data-access path. This leaves every call site responsible for preserving query structure.

**Architectural anti-pattern - Raw SQL string interpolation.** Query strings are assembled from request values on several routes, so one parameterized fix does not close the pattern; data access needs a safe-by-default layer.

**Confirmed findings:**

- 🔴 [F-005](#f-005) — SQL injection in product search query
- 🔴 [F-010](#f-010) — SQL injection in login query through the email field

**Practice sites:**

- `routes/login.ts:34`
- `routes/search.ts:23`

**Architecture evidence:** Parameterized Queries, ORM / Repository Layer, Source evidence (`routes/vulnCodeSnippet.ts:46`)

**Affected components:** [C-03](#c-03), [C-09](#c-09)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — provide one parameterised repository or query-builder path and prohibit application-value interpolation in database queries
- **Tactical** — ● [M-185](#m-185) — Use parameterized replacements in search query, ● [M-186](#m-186) — Use parameterized queries for login lookup

<a id="w-003"></a>
### W-003 — Authentication relies on hand-rolled token verification

🟠 High · implementation weakness · confirmed · 8 findings

Token validation is implemented in application code rather than behind one vetted and consistently configured authentication path. Algorithm, issuer, audience, and key-handling checks can diverge between callers.

**Confirmed findings:**

- 🟠 [F-013](#f-013) — Chatbot identity taken from an unverified JWT
- 🟠 [F-014](#f-014) — JWT verify call missing algorithms allowlist
- 🟡 [F-078](#f-078) — Review author taken from request body

**Practice sites:**

- 🟠 [F-019](#f-019) — JWT verification does not pin the accepted signature
- 🟠 [F-076](#f-076) — JWT verification does not pin the expected algorithm
- 🟡 [F-083](#f-083) — OAuth login trusts userinfo without checking token audience
- 🟡 [F-086](#f-086) — Token checks omit issuer and audience, and the jws path
- 🟡 [F-091](#f-091) — Published container images lack signature and provenance attestation

**Affected components:** [C-02](#c-02), [C-03](#c-03), [C-08](#c-08), [C-09](#c-09)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — centralise token validation on a vetted library with pinned algorithms, issuer and audience checks, and managed signing keys
- **Tactical** — ◕ [M-188](#m-188) — Verify JWT signature before trusting chat identity, ◕ [M-160](#m-160) — Pin JWT verification to RS256, ◑ [M-244](#m-244) — Verify token signatures before trusting claims, ◕ [M-191](#m-191) — Pin RS256 in every token verification call, ◑ [M-173](#m-173) — Manual review: verify JWT verification does not pin the expected algorithm, ◕ [M-242](#m-242) — Verify tokens with jsonwebtoken pinned to RS256, ◑ [M-174](#m-174) — Manual review: verify OAuth login trusts userinfo without checking token audience, ◑ [M-249](#m-249) — Enforce JWT signature and algorithm verification (F-083), ◑ [M-252](#m-252) — Enforce JWT signature and algorithm verification (F-086), ◑ [M-180](#m-180) — Sign and verify release artifacts

<a id="w-004"></a>
### W-004 — Input handling lacks enforced boundary validation

🟠 High · design weakness · confirmed · 2 findings

Request handlers do not validate input against one enforced server-side schema or allowlist at the boundary - validation is either absent on the vulnerable parameters or limited to rejecting selected bad patterns (a blacklist). New encodings and unanticipated input forms can therefore reach downstream parsers or interpreters.

**Confirmed findings:**

- 🟠 [F-026](#f-026) — Data erasure layout parameter selects an arbitrary

**Practice sites:**

- 🟠 [F-027](#f-027) — Path traversal in ZIP upload extraction writes outside

**Architecture evidence:** Schema Validation, Allowlist Validation, Source evidence (`server.ts:204`), Source evidence (`server.ts:246`), Source evidence (`frontend/src/app/photo-wall/photo-wall.component.ts:93`), Source evidence (`frontend/src/hacking-instructor/helpers/helpers.ts:262`) (+4 more)

**Affected components:** [C-03](#c-03)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — enforce server-side schemas and domain-specific allowlists before input reaches parsing, persistence, or command construction
- **Tactical** — ◕ [M-198](#m-198) — Constrain file paths to a safe base directory (F-026), ◑ [M-167](#m-167) — Manual review: verify Path traversal in ZIP upload extraction writes outside the target…, ◕ [M-199](#m-199) — Confine extracted entries to the upload directory

Authorization depends on per-handler checks instead of a policy boundary that consistently enforces role, ownership, and tenant scope. New routes can bypass protection by omitting a local check.

<a id="w-006"></a>
### W-006 — Privileged attributes lack an enforced write allowlist

🟠 High · implementation weakness · confirmed · 5 findings

Model binding accepts caller-selected attributes across a privileged write boundary. The affected binding path does not separate fields callers may change from fields reserved for server-side policy.

**Architectural anti-pattern - Mass-assignment / unscoped object binding.** Generated create and update handlers bind the whole request body to models, so privileged fields such as role are client-writable without a write allowlist.

**Confirmed findings:**

- 🟠 [F-071](#f-071) — Self-registration accepts a client-supplied admin role
- 🟠 [F-072](#f-072) — Self-registration can set the admin role through the request

**Practice sites:**

- 🟠 [F-061](#f-061) — User role attribute assignable
- 🟡 [F-100](#f-100) — Basket item update accepts client-supplied BasketId
- 🟡 [F-169](#f-169) — Owner field UserId accepted from the request body on generated

**Affected components:** [C-01](#c-01), [C-03](#c-03)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — define operation-specific write schemas and assign privileged attributes exclusively through server-side policy
- **Tactical** — ● [M-237](#m-237) — Strip privileged fields from registration requests, ● [M-238](#m-238) — Strip or server-assign role on user registration, ◑ [M-171](#m-171) — Manual review: verify User role attribute assignable through mass assignment, ◕ [M-228](#m-228) — Restrict role assignment to an admin-only code path, ◑ [M-264](#m-264) — Allow-list updatable basket item fields, ◕ [M-331](#m-331) — Set the owner server-side and allow-list create fields

<a id="w-007"></a>
### W-007 — Frontend rendering lacks enforced output encoding

🟠 High · design weakness · confirmed · 17 findings

Browser rendering paths write HTML or DOM content directly without one enforced contextual-encoding or sanitisation boundary. Safety depends on each individual component using the right API.

**Architectural anti-pattern - SPA without BFF.** The Angular client holds the session token where page scripts can read it, and no server-side session holder exists, so every XSS becomes session theft.

- 🟠 [F-043](#f-043) — DOM XSS
- 🟠 [F-044](#f-044) — DOM-based XSS

- 🟡 [F-105](#f-105) — Stored XSS

<a id="w-008"></a>
### W-008 — Document-database queries take their structure from application values

🟠 High · implementation weakness · observed-practice · 3 findings

Document-store queries take operators or JavaScript predicates from application values instead of fixed query structures with typed values. Callers can change what a query selects, for example through injected operators or evaluated expressions.

**Practice sites:**

- 🟠 [F-031](#f-031) — NoSQL \$where injection
- 🟠 [F-032](#f-032) — Request input in executable MongoDB predicates
- 🟠 [F-033](#f-033) — NoSQL \$where injection in order tracking

**Affected components:** [C-03](#c-03)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — build document queries from fixed operator structures, coerce request values to the expected scalar types, and disable server-side JavaScript evaluation in the database
- **Tactical** — ◑ [M-168](#m-168) — Manual review: verify NoSQL \$where injection through product id in review listing, ◕ [M-201](#m-201) — Query reviews by numeric product equality, ◕ [M-202](#m-202) — Use typed query predicates instead of \$where, ◑ [M-169](#m-169) — Manual review: verify NoSQL \$where injection in order tracking, ◕ [M-203](#m-203) — Replace \$where string with equality query on orderId

<a id="w-009"></a>
### W-009 — Browser scripts retain reusable session credentials

🟠 High · implementation weakness · observed-practice · 1 finding

Browser code retains reusable authentication credentials in script-readable storage. Script execution in the same origin can obtain the credentials and reuse the authenticated session.

**Practice sites:**

- 🟠 [F-059](#f-059) — Session JWT and 2FA token stored in localStorage and script-readable cookie

**Affected components:** [C-08](#c-08)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — move reusable credentials behind a server-managed session boundary and keep browser-visible state free of reusable authentication secrets
- **Tactical** — ◕ [M-226](#m-226) — Store session tokens in HttpOnly, Secure cookies

<a id="w-010"></a>
### W-010 — Secrets are committed to source instead of a managed store

🟠 High · design weakness · design-risk · 8 findings

Cryptographic keys, credentials, and other high-entropy secrets are embedded as literals in source or config rather than resolved at runtime from a managed secret store, so anyone with repository read access obtains reusable signing material and credentials.

**Architectural anti-pattern - Secrets hardcoded in source.** The token signing key is committed to the repository rather than loaded from a managed store, so anyone with source access can forge sessions.

**Practice sites:**

- 🟠 [F-016](#f-016) — RSA token signing key is hardcoded in source
- 🟠 [F-022](#f-022) — Hardcoded RSA private key used to sign session JWTs
- 🟠 [F-058](#f-058) — User account passwords embedded in shipped client tutorial
- 🟡 [F-085](#f-085) — Hardcoded testing account credentials in client bundle
- 🟡 [F-128](#f-128) — Cookie signing secret hardcoded in server setup
- 🟡 [F-131](#f-131) — Hardcoded wallet mnemonic exposes Ethereum private key
- 🟡 [F-139](#f-139) — HMAC key for security answers hardcoded in source
- 🟡 [F-140](#f-140) — Seeded account passwords embedded in the login handler

**Architecture evidence:** Managed Secret Store, Runtime Secret Injection, Source evidence (`routes/checkKeys.ts:10`), Source evidence (`lib/insecurity.ts:21`)

**Affected components:** [C-03](#c-03), [C-04](#c-04), [C-08](#c-08), [C-09](#c-09)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — move every secret to a managed secret store or injected environment configuration, rotate the exposed values, and add secret-scanning to CI
- **Tactical** — ◕ [M-190](#m-190) — Move cryptographic keys to a managed secret store, ● [M-194](#m-194) — Load JWT signing key from a secret store, ◕ [M-225](#m-225) — Remove account passwords from client bundle, ◑ [M-251](#m-251) — Remove embedded test credentials from the client, ◑ [M-292](#m-292) — Load cookie signing secret from secret store, ◑ [M-295](#m-295) — Move wallet key material to a secret store, ◑ [M-303](#m-303) — Move the HMAC key into managed secret storage, ◑ [M-304](#m-304) — Remove cleartext account passwords from source code

<a id="w-011"></a>

🟡 Medium · design weakness · confirmed · 1 finding

Sensitive API routes and real-time channels are exposed without an enforced authentication check at the endpoint boundary. Access control depends on each handler (or the caller) remembering to require a session, so an unauthenticated client can reach privileged operations directly.

**Confirmed findings:**

- 🟡 [F-132](#f-132) — Unauthenticated WebSocket Channel

**Architecture evidence:** Route Authentication Middleware, Server-Side Session Enforcement, Source evidence (`server.ts:309`), Source evidence (`server.ts:312`), Source evidence (`server.ts:402`), Source evidence (`server.ts:404`) (+4 more)

**Affected components:** [C-03](#c-03), [C-06](#c-06)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — enforce authentication centrally at the routing and channel boundary so every exposed endpoint requires a verified session unless explicitly marked public
- **Tactical** — ◑ [M-296](#m-296) — Require authentication on every exposed endpoint

<a id="w-018"></a>
### W-018 — Application data crosses into template source

🟡 Medium · implementation weakness · observed-practice · 2 findings

Template construction treats application values as template source. Rendering can therefore interpret data as template instructions.

**Practice sites:**

- 🟠 [F-035](#f-035) — Input compiled as template source
- 🟠 [F-074](#f-074) — Server-side template injection

**Affected components:** [C-03](#c-03)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — compile fixed templates and pass application values only through their data-binding interface
- **Tactical** — ◕ [M-161](#m-161) — Render username as escaped Pug local, ◕ [M-240](#m-240) — Remove server-side evaluation of untrusted input (F-074)

<a id="w-024"></a>
### W-024 — OAuth sign-in requests are not bound to the user's browser session

🟡 Medium · implementation weakness · observed-practice · 1 finding

The client starts OAuth or OpenID Connect sign-in without a per-request state value that its callback verifies. A forged authorization response can then complete sign-in in the victim's browser with the attacker's grant.

**Practice sites:**

- 🟡 [F-084](#f-084) — OAuth implicit flow without state, nonce or PKCE

**Affected components:** [C-08](#c-08)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — start every sign-in through a maintained OAuth/OIDC client that generates and verifies state (and PKCE) for each authorization request
- **Tactical** — ◑ [M-250](#m-250) — Add anti-CSRF protection to state-changing requests (F-084)

<a id="w-025"></a>
### W-025 — Cookie-authenticated changes lack request authenticity checks

🟡 Medium · implementation weakness · observed-practice · 3 findings

A state-changing operation accepts ambient browser credentials without an enforced check that the authenticated user initiated the request. The affected mutation path lacks a request-authenticity boundary.

**Practice sites:**

- 🟡 [F-096](#f-096) — Data erasure request without re-verification
- 🟡 [F-097](#f-097) — Cross-site request forgery on profile
- 🟡 [F-110](#f-110) — Chat auto-sends attacker-supplied

**Affected components:** [C-03](#c-03), [C-08](#c-08)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — enforce framework CSRF protection or validated origin and anti-CSRF tokens on every mutation that accepts ambient browser credentials
- **Tactical** — ◑ [M-260](#m-260) — Verify security answer and CSRF token before erasure, ◑ [M-261](#m-261) — Add CSRF token and SameSite=Strict session cookie, ◑ [M-274](#m-274) — Add anti-CSRF protection to state-changing requests (F-110)

<a id="w-026"></a>

🟡 Medium · implementation weakness · observed-practice · 1 finding

**Practice sites:**

- 🟡 [F-093](#f-093) — Untrusted npm Install/Postinstall Scripts Enabled

**Affected components:** [C-02](#c-02)

**Remediation:**

- **Tactical** — ◑ [M-162](#m-162) — Install npm dependencies with --ignore-scripts in the `Dockerfile`

<a id="w-027"></a>
### W-027 — Sensitive cookies remain accessible to browser scripts

🟡 Medium · implementation weakness · observed-practice · 1 finding

Sensitive cookies are issued without HttpOnly protection. Browser scripts in the same origin can read values that should remain confined to the browser's HTTP cookie handling.

**Practice sites:**

- 🟡 [F-141](#f-141) — Session token cookie set without HttpOnly or Secure

**Affected components:** [C-09](#c-09)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — issue sensitive cookies server-side with HttpOnly protection and keep script-readable application preferences separate from session credentials
- **Tactical** — ◑ [M-305](#m-305) — Set secure session-cookie attributes

<a id="w-041"></a>
### W-041 — Security-sensitive data uses weak cryptographic primitives

🟡 Medium · implementation weakness · observed-practice · 3 findings

Password, token, or integrity protection uses a weak hash, predictable random source, or insufficient work factor. The application may use a standard library, but the selected primitive does not provide the required security property.

**Practice sites:**

- 🟠 [F-047](#f-047) — Passwords stored and compared as unsalted MD5
- 🟠 [F-056](#f-056) — Passwords hashed and compared as unsalted MD5
- 🟡 [F-113](#f-113) — Security answers hashed with a fast HMAC

**Affected components:** [C-01](#c-01), [C-03](#c-03)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — standardise on a password KDF, a CSPRNG for secrets, and modern authenticated cryptographic primitives with centrally reviewed parameters
- **Tactical** — ◕ [M-216](#m-216) — Hash passwords with bcrypt or argon2id, ◕ [M-223](#m-223) — Store passwords with bcrypt or argon2id, ◑ [M-277](#m-277) — Hash security answers with a salted adaptive KDF

<a id="w-042"></a>
### W-042 — Build pipeline trusts mutable third-party references

🟡 Medium · design weakness · design-risk · 4 findings

CI/CD workflows resolve third-party actions and other build dependencies to mutable tags or branches instead of immutable commit digests, so a retagged or compromised upstream runs inside the pipeline with its token and secret scope.

**Practice sites:**

- 🟠 [F-024](#f-024) — GitHub Actions referenced by mutable tags or branches
- 🟠 [F-025](#f-025) — Lockfile generation disabled by npm configuration — .npmrc:1
- 🟡 [F-094](#f-094) — Container base images referenced by floating tags without digest
- 🟡 [F-095](#f-095) — Release job installs global build tools and dependencies

**Architecture evidence:** Commit-SHA Action Pinning, Dependency Provenance Verification, Source evidence (`.github/workflows/image_actions.yml:33`), Source evidence (`.github/workflows/image_actions.yml:42`), Source evidence (`.github/workflows/ci.yml:188`)

**Affected components:** [C-02](#c-02)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — pin every third-party action and build dependency to an immutable commit SHA (or a vetted internal mirror) and enforce SHA-pinning in CI
- **Tactical** — ◕ [M-196](#m-196) — Pin image-actions and other actions to commit SHAs, ◕ [M-197](#m-197) — Commit lockfiles and install with npm ci, ◑ [M-258](#m-258) — Pin base images by sha256 digest, ◑ [M-259](#m-259) — Pin exact tool versions and use npm ci

## 8. Findings Register

Findings are grouped by severity (Critical → High → Medium → Low); within a tier they are ordered by attack vektor (Repo-Read → Internet-Anon → Internet-User → Victim-Required). Every finding card shows **Severity · Component · Location**, **Issue**, **Fix**, and **Classification** (with external CWE / OWASP links). **Evidence**, **Root cause**, violated requirements, the weakness, and the trust boundary gap appear when they apply.

**Risk Distribution:** 🔴 Critical: 5 · 🟠 High: 61 · 🟡 Medium: 96 · 🟢 Low: n/a · **Total findings: 162**
*This register counts every finding card below. The Management Summary's risk distribution (Total: 79) counts folded insecure-practice sites under their weakness and adds each design risk once.*

### 🔴 Critical (5)

<a id="t-001"></a><a id="f-001"></a>
#### F-001 · Attacker-Controlled Owner ID Authorization Bypass

**Severity:** 🔴 Critical  ·  **Component:** [C-03](#c-03) - Juice Shop Express API  ·  **Location:** Multiple locations (5)

**Contradicted by verification:** a control was evidenced at [TC-AUTHZ-01](#tc-authz-01) at `routes/memory.ts:15`; [TC-AUTHZ-01](#tc-authz-01) at `routes/payment.ts:41`; [TC-AUTHZ-01](#tc-authz-01) at `routes/wallet.ts:12`; [TC-AUTHZ-01](#tc-authz-01) at `routes/wallet.ts:24`; [TC-AUTHZ-01](#tc-authz-01) at `routes/wallet.ts:27`. Check this finding before acting on it.

**Reliability:** Low. This may be a false positive: a review or check contradicts it.

**Weakness:** [W-005](#w-005) - Authorization is implemented route by route

**Instances (5):** `routes/memory.ts`: 15; `routes/payment.ts`: 41; `routes/wallet.ts`: 24, 12, 27

**Issue:** A signed-in attacker puts another user's ID into the owner field of the request (`req.body.UserId` and the like); the query trusts it instead of the session identity, so the attacker reads or changes every other user's records - horizontal privilege escalation across all accounts.

**Evidence:** ◌ ambiguous

```typescript
routes/memory.ts
  12 │     const record = {
  13 │       caption: req.body.caption,
  14 │       imagePath: 'assets/public/images/uploads/' + req.file?.filename,
→ 15 │       UserId: req.body.UserId
  16 │     }
  17 │     const memory = await MemoryModel.create(record)
  18 │     res.status(200).json({ status: 'success', data: memory })
```

**Fix:** Tie every object lookup to the requesting user's identity and reject cross-tenant references → ◑ [M-165](#m-165) — Manual review: verify Attacker-Controlled Owner ID Authorization Bypass · ● [M-181](#m-181) — Enforce object-level (ownership) authorization (🔴 [F-001](#f-001))

**Classification:** Broken Access Control · STRIDE: Tampering · [CWE-639](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/639.html) · [OWASP A01:2025](https://owasp.org/Top10/2025/A01_2025-Broken_Access_Control/) · Verification items: [TC-AUTHZ-01](#tc-authz-01) · Rules: AUTHZ-001 · walkthrough [Walkthrough §3.2](#32-attacker-controlled-owner-id-authorization-bypass-in-memory)

<a id="t-004"></a><a id="f-004"></a>
#### F-004 · OAuth account password derived from public email address

**Severity:** 🔴 Critical  ·  **Component:** [C-08](#c-08) - Angular Client Application  ·  **Location:** `frontend/src/app/oauth/oauth.component.ts:30`

**Found by:** survey and verification ([TC-AUTHN-07](#tc-authn-07))

**Reliability:** High. Probably real: a model review confirmed it, and the survey and verification each found it.

**Issue:** At `frontend/src/app/oauth/oauth.component.ts:30` the client registers and logs in every OAuth user with the password `btoa(reversed email)`, which is base64 of the reversed email string. An attacker learns the email of a user linked to Google sign-in, for example from reviews or other public profile data.

The attacker computes the password and POSTs the email and password to the public `/rest/user/login` endpoint. The server checks only email and password and does not tell OAuth logins from password logins, so the attacker gets a valid JWT for the victim's account without Google.

Any OAuth-linked account, including privileged ones, can be fully taken over by anyone who knows its email.

**Evidence:** ✓ verified - `oauth.component.ts:30` sets the password to `btoa(email reversed)` and saves the user; line 46 logs in with the same derived value. `routes/login.ts:34` authenticates on email plus password hash alone and ignores the oauth flag.

```typescript
  27 │   ngOnInit (): void {
  28 │     this.userService.oauthLogin(this.parseRedirectUrlParams().access_token).subscribe({
  29 │       next: (profile: any) => {
→ 30 │         const password = btoa(profile.email.split('').reverse().join(''))
  31 │         this.userService.save({ email: profile.email, password, passwordRepeat: password }).subscribe({
  32 │           next: () => {
  33 │             this.login(profile)
```

**Fix:** ● [M-184](#m-184) — Exchange OAuth tokens server-side instead of derived passwords

**Classification:** OAuth / OIDC Misconfiguration · STRIDE: Spoofing · [CWE-1391](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/1391.html) · [OWASP A07:2025](https://owasp.org/Top10/2025/A07_2025-Authentication_Failures/) · Verification items: [TC-AUTHN-07](#tc-authn-07) · walkthrough [Walkthrough §3.5](#35-oauth-account-password-derived-from-public-email-address)

<a id="t-005"></a><a id="f-005"></a>
#### F-005 · SQL injection in product search query

**Severity:** 🔴 Critical  ·  **Component:** [C-03](#c-03) - Juice Shop Express API  ·  **Location:** `routes/search.ts:23`

**Found by:** survey, scanners and verification ([TC-INPUT-01](#tc-input-01))

**Reliability:** High. Probably real: a model review confirmed it, and the survey and verification each found it.

**Weakness:** [W-002](#w-002) - Database access relies on concatenated queries

**Issue:** An attacker who does not need to log in sends GET `/rest/products/search?q=` with a payload such as qwert`')) UNION SELECT id,email,password,'`4','5','6','7','8','9' FROM Users--. At `routes/search.ts:23` the handler puts the q value straight into a raw `sequelize.query` string, inside the LIKE clause.

The query runs with the payload and returns the results as product rows. The survey part also notes that the route has no authentication (`server.ts:602`).

**Evidence:** ✓ verified

```typescript
  20 │   return (req: Request, res: Response, next: NextFunction) => {
  21 │     let criteria: any = req.query.q === 'undefined' ? '' : req.query.q ?? ''
  22 │     criteria = (criteria.length <= 200) ? criteria : criteria.substring(0, 200)
→ 23 │     models.sequelize.query(`SELECT * FROM Products WHERE ((name LIKE '%${criteria}%' OR description LIKE '%${criteria}%') AND deletedAt IS NULL) ORDER BY name`) // vuln-code-snippet vuln-line unionSqlInjectionChallenge dbSchemaChallenge
  24 │       .then(([products]: any) => {
  25 │         const dataString = JSON.stringify(products)
  26 │         if (challengeUtils.notSolved(challenges.unionSqlInjectionChallenge)) { // vuln-code-snippet hide-start
```

**Fix:** Switch all SQL execution to parameterised queries or ORM-bound parameters → ● [M-185](#m-185) — Use parameterized replacements in search query

**Classification:** Injection · STRIDE: Tampering · [CWE-89](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/89.html) · [OWASP A05:2025](https://owasp.org/Top10/2025/A05_2025-Injection/) · Verification items: [TC-INPUT-01](#tc-input-01) · Rules: INJ-001 · walkthrough [Walkthrough §3.3](#33-sql-injection-in-product-search-query)

<a id="t-010"></a><a id="f-010"></a>
#### F-010 · SQL injection in login query through the email field

**Severity:** 🔴 Critical  ·  **Component:** [C-09](#c-09) - Authentication & Session Surface  ·  **Location:** `routes/login.ts:34`

**Found by:** survey, scanners and verification ([TC-INPUT-01](#tc-input-01))

**Reliability:** High. Probably real: a model review confirmed it, and the survey and verification each found it.

**Weakness:** [W-002](#w-002) - Database access relies on concatenated queries

**Trust boundary gap:** [tb-5](#tb-5) - Authentication &amp; Session Surface → SQLite Embedded Database · Query construction: The request email becomes SQL text at the login query crossing into SQLite, breaking the data-interpretation assumption.

**Issue:** An attacker who does not need to log in posts to `/rest/user/login` with an email value such as admin@juice-`sh.op`'-- and any password. At `routes/login.ts:34` the handler places `req.body.email` inside the raw SQL text and runs it through `models.sequelize.query` with no bound parameters.

The quote closes the email literal, and the comment removes the password condition, so the query returns the targeted row (or, per the survey part, any first user). The handler then signs a JWT for that row through `security.authorize`.

Full account takeover of any user including administrators without credentials.

**Evidence:** ✓ verified

```typescript
  31 │
  32 │   return (req: Request, res: Response, next: NextFunction) => {
  33 │     verifyPreLoginChallenges(req) // vuln-code-snippet hide-line
→ 34 │     models.sequelize.query(`SELECT * FROM Users WHERE email = '${req.body.email || ''}' AND password = '${security.hash(req.body.password || '')}' AND deletedAt IS NULL`, { model: UserModel, plain: true }) // vuln-code-snippet vuln-line loginAdminChallenge loginBenderChallenge loginJimChallenge
  35 │       .then((authenticatedUser) => { // vuln-code-snippet neutral-line loginAdminChallenge loginBenderChallenge loginJimChallenge
  36 │         const user = utils.queryResultToJson(authenticatedUser)
  37 │         if (user.data?.id && user.data.totpSecret !== '') {
```

**Fix:** Switch all SQL execution to parameterised queries or ORM-bound parameters → ● [M-186](#m-186) — Use parameterized queries for login lookup

**Classification:** Injection · STRIDE: Tampering · [CWE-89](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/89.html) · [OWASP A05:2025](https://owasp.org/Top10/2025/A05_2025-Injection/) · Verification items: [TC-INPUT-01](#tc-input-01) · Rules: INJ-001 · walkthrough [Walkthrough §3.4](#34-sql-injection-in-login-query)

<a id="t-011"></a><a id="f-011"></a>
#### F-011 · Server-side code execution

**Severity:** 🔴 Critical - verified attack-chain keystone in AC-T-006 (Remote Code Execution via Server-Side Injection); see [§9](#9-abuse-cases)  ·  **Component:** [C-03](#c-03) - Juice Shop Express API  ·  **Location:** `routes/userProfile.ts:61`

**Found by:** survey, scanners and verification ([TC-INPUT-03](#tc-input-03))

**Reliability:** High. Probably real: a model review confirmed it, and the survey and verification each found it.

**Weakness:** [W-001](#w-001) - Application data crosses into executable code

**Issue:** A registered user sets their username through POST `/profile` to a value matching the #{...} pattern, such as #{`global.process.mainModule.require('child_process').execSync('id').toString()`}. The survey reports that the value is stored without validation (`updateUserProfile.ts:38`).

The persisted user record is read. The survey describes a working attack, the verification part says a user who can set such a username could run code, and the scanner observed only the unsafe path and says attacker control, access prerequisites and active configuration still need verification.

**Evidence:** ✓ verified

```typescript
  58 │         if (!code) {
  59 │           throw new Error('Username is null')
  60 │         }
→ 61 │         username = eval(code) // eslint-disable-line no-eval
  62 │       } catch (err) {
  63 │         username = '\\' + username
  64 │       }
```

**Fix:** Replace runtime code generation (eval/Function/template render) with a data-only execution path → ● [M-187](#m-187) — Remove server-side evaluation of untrusted input (🔴 [F-011](#f-011))

**Classification:** Code Execution via Unsafe Deserialization or Eval · STRIDE: Elevation of Privilege · [CWE-95](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/95.html) · [OWASP A08:2025](https://owasp.org/Top10/2025/A08_2025-Software_or_Data_Integrity_Failures/) · Verification items: [TC-INPUT-03](#tc-input-03) · Rules: INJ-NODE-007 · walkthrough [Walkthrough §3.1](#31-server-side-code-execution-in-user-profile)

### 🟠 High (61)

<a id="t-016"></a><a id="f-016"></a>
#### F-016 · RSA token signing key is hardcoded in source

**Severity:** 🟠 High _(raw Critical)_  ·  **Component:** [C-09](#c-09) - Authentication & Session Surface  ·  **Location:** `lib/insecurity.ts:54`

**Found by:** verification ([TC-AUTHN-02](#tc-authn-02))

**Reliability:** Medium. A model review confirmed it, but the survey and verification did not both find it.

**Weakness:** [W-010](#w-010) - Secrets are committed to source instead of a managed store

**Issue:** Anyone who can read the source or a build artifact can take the private key literal and sign a token with arbitrary data, such as an admin user. `isAuthorized` and `updateAuthenticatedUsers` accept that token as genuine.

**Evidence:** ✓ verified - Line 21 holds the PEM private key literal, and authorize at line 54 signs every session token with it.

**Fix:** Move the cryptographic key out of source control into a managed secret store and rotate it → ◕ [M-190](#m-190) — Move cryptographic keys to a managed secret store

**Classification:** Cryptographic Failures · STRIDE: Spoofing · [CWE-321](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/321.html) · [OWASP A04:2025](https://owasp.org/Top10/2025/A04_2025-Cryptographic_Failures/) · Verification items: [TC-AUTHN-02](#tc-authn-02)

<a id="t-022"></a><a id="f-022"></a>
#### F-022 · Hardcoded RSA private key used to sign session JWTs

**Severity:** 🟠 High - secret committed to the public source repo - extractable on clone, no prior access needed; elevated to Critical as a verified attack-chain keystone in AC-T-005 (Authentication Bypass via Exposed Secret Material); see [§9](#9-abuse-cases) _(raw Critical)_  ·  **Component:** [C-09](#c-09) - Authentication & Session Surface  ·  **Location:** `lib/insecurity.ts:21`

**Found by:** survey and verification ([TC-DATA-03](#tc-data-03))

**Reliability:** High. Probably real: a model review confirmed it, and the survey and verification each found it.

**Weakness:** [W-010](#w-010) - Secrets are committed to source instead of a managed store

**Issue:** The RSA private key that signs session JWTs is a source literal. Anyone who obtains the source, for example from a public repository, an image or a leaked build, can use the key to sign arbitrary JWTs with any user id and role, such as admin.

`isAuthorized()` accepts these tokens because they verify against the matching public key. Forged tokens impersonate any user or role across all JWT-protected routes.

**Evidence:** ✓ verified

```typescript
  19 │
  20 │ export const publicKey = fs ? fs.readFileSync('encryptionkeys/jwt.pub', 'utf8') : 'placeholder-public-key'
→ 21 │ const privateKey = '[PEM PRIVATE KEY — REDACTED] …
  22 │
  23 │ interface ResponseWithUser {
```

**Fix:** Move the credential out of source control into a secret store and rotate it → ● [M-194](#m-194) — Load JWT signing key from a secret store

**Classification:** Cryptographic Failures · STRIDE: Spoofing · [CWE-798](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/798.html) · [OWASP A04:2025](https://owasp.org/Top10/2025/A04_2025-Cryptographic_Failures/) · Verification items: [TC-DATA-03](#tc-data-03) · walkthrough [Walkthrough §3.7](#37-hardcoded-rsa-private-key-used-to-sign-session-jwts)

<a id="t-046"></a><a id="f-046"></a>
#### F-046 · Payment card numbers stored in cleartext in SQLite

**Severity:** 🟠 High  ·  **Component:** [C-01](#c-01) - Sequelize application data access  ·  **Location:** `models/card.ts:38`

**Found by:** survey and verification ([TC-DATA-02](#tc-data-02))

**Reliability:** High. Probably real: a model review confirmed it, and the survey and verification each found it.

**Weakness:** [W-031](#w-031) - Payment card numbers stored in cleartext in SQLite (`card.ts:38`)

**Issue:** At `models/card.ts:38`, the Cards model stores full card numbers and expiry dates in plain form, with only range validation. `models/index.ts:41` configures plain SQLite storage with no encryption at rest.

An attacker who can read data/juiceshop.sqlite or a backup, or who exploits a query flaw, gets the full PAN of every stored card. Disclosure of full payment card numbers for all users.

**Evidence:** ✓ verified - `Card.cardNum` is a plain INTEGER column in the unencrypted SQLite file data/juiceshop.sqlite.

```typescript
  36 │       },
  37 │       fullName: DataTypes.STRING,
→ 38 │       cardNum: {
  39 │         type: DataTypes.INTEGER,
  40 │         validate: {
```

**Fix:** ◕ [M-215](#m-215) — Encrypt or tokenize stored card numbers

**Classification:** Cryptographic Failures · STRIDE: Information Disclosure · [CWE-312](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/312.html) · [OWASP A04:2025](https://owasp.org/Top10/2025/A04_2025-Cryptographic_Failures/) · Verification items: [TC-DATA-02](#tc-data-02)

<a id="t-058"></a><a id="f-058"></a>
#### F-058 · User account passwords embedded in shipped client tutorial

**Severity:** 🟠 High  ·  **Component:** [C-08](#c-08) - Angular Client Application  ·  **Location:** `frontend/src/hacking-instructor/challenges/loginBender.ts:84`

**Reliability:** Medium. A model review confirmed it, but the survey and verification did not both find it.

**Weakness:** [W-010](#w-010) - Secrets are committed to source instead of a managed store

**Issue:** The hacking-instructor modules are bundled into the public SPA. They contain literal passwords used to match login input: Bender's, Jim's 'ncc-1701' at `loginJim.ts:73`, the testing account's at `exposedCredentials.ts:60` and 'admin123' at `passwordStrength.ts:73`.

An anonymous visitor who reads `main.js` can recover these values and try them on the login endpoint to take over the matching accounts. Anyone can read these credentials.

If they match the seeded accounts, including admin, they give access to those accounts.

**Evidence:** ✓ verified - Tutorial hint resolvers compare form input against literal account passwords. `index.ts` imports these modules into the client bundle.

```typescript
  82 │       fixture: '#password',
  83 │       unskippable: true,
→ 84 │       resolved: waitForInputToNotHaveValueAndNotBeEmpty('#password', 'OhG0**** (24 chars)')
  85 │     },
  86 │     {
```

**Fix:** Move the credential out of source control into a secret store and rotate it → ◕ [M-225](#m-225) — Remove account passwords from client bundle

**Classification:** Cryptographic Failures · STRIDE: Information Disclosure · [CWE-798](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/798.html) · [OWASP A04:2025](https://owasp.org/Top10/2025/A04_2025-Cryptographic_Failures/)

<a id="t-002"></a><a id="f-002"></a>
#### F-002 · Basket and basket item endpoints lack ownership checks

**Severity:** 🟠 High  ·  **Component:** [C-03](#c-03) - Juice Shop Express API  ·  **Location:** Multiple locations (5)

**Found by:** survey and scanners

**Reliability:** Low. Not confirmed, this may be a false positive: Route is authenticated (359, `appendUserId`) but the `quantityCheckBeforeBasketItemUpdate` handler, where an ownership check could exist, is not supplied.

**Weakness:** [W-005](#w-005) - Authorization is implemented route by route

**Instances (5):** `routes/basketItems.ts`: 68; `server.ts`: 426, 603, 604, 605

**Issue:** An authenticated user supplies another user's id to basket endpoints that enforce authentication but no ownership predicate. At `routes/basketItems.ts:68`, `quantityCheckBeforeBasketItemUpdate` loads the item by the path id and passes the request to the generic finale BasketItem update.

The same missing ownership check lets a user read GET `/rest/basket/:id` (`server.ts:603`), call POST `/rest/basket/:id/checkout` (`server.ts:604`), and call PUT `/rest/basket/:id/coupon/:coupon` (`server.ts:605`) on another user's basket.

**Evidence:** ◌ ambiguous

```typescript
server.ts
  424 │   app.use('/b2b/v2', security.isAuthorized())
  425 │   /* Check if the quantity is available in stock and limit per user not exceeded, then add item to basket */
→ 426 │   app.put('/api/BasketItems/:id', security.appendUserId(), utils.asyncHandler(basketItems.quantityCheckBeforeBasketItemUpdate()))
  427 │   app.post('/api/BasketItems', security.appendUserId(), utils.asyncHandler(basketItems.quantityCheckBeforeBasketItemAddition()), utils.asyncHandler(basketItems.addBasketItem()))
  428 │   /* Accounting users are allowed to check and update quantities */
```

**Fix:** Tie every object lookup to the requesting user's identity and reject cross-tenant references → ◑ [M-166](#m-166) — Manual review: verify Basket and basket item endpoints lack ownership checks · ◕ [M-182](#m-182) — Enforce object-level (ownership) authorization (🟠 [F-002](#f-002))

**Classification:** Broken Access Control · STRIDE: Elevation of Privilege · [CWE-639](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/639.html) · [OWASP A01:2025](https://owasp.org/Top10/2025/A01_2025-Broken_Access_Control/) · Verification items: [TC-AUTHZ-01](#tc-authz-01) · Rules: AUTHZ-301

<a id="t-003"></a><a id="f-003"></a>
#### F-003 · Missing ownership check on resource IDs

**Severity:** 🟠 High  ·  **Component:** [C-03](#c-03) - Juice Shop Express API  ·  **Location:** Multiple locations (3)

**Found by:** survey and verification ([TC-AUTHZ-01](#tc-authz-01))

**Reliability:** High. Probably real: a model review confirmed it, and the survey and verification each found it.

**Weakness:** [W-005](#w-005) - Authorization is implemented route by route

**Trust boundary gap:** [tb-1](#tb-1) 🌐 External - external → Juice Shop Express API · Authorization: Boundary assumes handlers check basket ownership against the authenticated subject; `placeOrder` uses the client-supplied id only.

**Instances (3):** `server.ts`: 450; `routes/coupon.ts`: 18; `routes/order.ts`: 35

**Issue:** Requests that name a resource by id do not check that the caller owns it. At `server.ts:450`, PUT `/api/Addresss/:id` runs only `appendUserId` and then falls through to the generic finale Address resource. The finding holds 3 places, more than the locations cited here.

**Evidence:** ✓ verified

```typescript
server.ts
  448 │   app.post('/api/Addresss', security.appendUserId())
  449 │   app.get('/api/Addresss', security.appendUserId(), utils.asyncHandler(address.getAddress()))
→ 450 │   app.put('/api/Addresss/:id', security.appendUserId())
  451 │   app.delete('/api/Addresss/:id', security.appendUserId(), utils.asyncHandler(address.delAddressById()))
  452 │   app.get('/api/Addresss/:id', security.appendUserId(), utils.asyncHandler(address.getAddressById()))
```

**Fix:** Tie every object lookup to the requesting user's identity and reject cross-tenant references → ◕ [M-183](#m-183) — Scope address updates to the authenticated owner

**Classification:** Broken Access Control · STRIDE: Elevation of Privilege · [CWE-639](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/639.html) · [OWASP A01:2025](https://owasp.org/Top10/2025/A01_2025-Broken_Access_Control/) · Verification items: [TC-AUTHZ-01](#tc-authz-01)

<a id="t-013"></a><a id="f-013"></a>
#### F-013 · Chatbot identity taken from an unverified JWT

**Severity:** 🟠 High  ·  **Component:** [C-03](#c-03) - Juice Shop Express API  ·  **Location:** `routes/chat.ts:45`

**Found by:** survey and verification ([TC-AI-03](#tc-ai-03), [TC-AI-04](#tc-ai-04))

**Reliability:** High. Probably real: a model review confirmed it, and the survey and verification each found it.

**Weakness:** [W-003](#w-003) - Authentication relies on hand-rolled token verification

**Trust boundary gap:** [tb-1](#tb-1) 🌐 External - external → Juice Shop Express API · Authentication: The chat handler accepts the caller's identity from an unverified token at the browser-to-API boundary, which bypasses the `isAuthorized()` verification that boundary assumes.

**Issue:** At `routes/chat.ts:45`, `getUserId` reads the bearer token with `security.decode` (`jws.decode`), which never verifies the signature. `/rest/chat` has no `isAuthorized` guard. One part adds that masking vowels lets different emails collide.

**Evidence:** ✓ verified

```typescript
  43 │   const token = utils.jwtFrom(req)
  44 │   if (!token) return undefined
→ 45 │   const decoded = security.decode(token) as { data?: { id?: number } } | undefined
  46 │   return decoded?.data?.id
  47 │ }
```

**Fix:** Pin the signature algorithm explicitly and reject `alg:none` and unknown algorithms → ◕ [M-188](#m-188) — Verify JWT signature before trusting chat identity

**Classification:** Broken Authentication · STRIDE: Spoofing · [CWE-347](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/347.html) · [OWASP A07:2025](https://owasp.org/Top10/2025/A07_2025-Authentication_Failures/) · Verification items: [TC-AI-03](#tc-ai-03), [TC-AI-04](#tc-ai-04)



**Classification:** Broken Authentication · STRIDE: Spoofing · [CWE-620](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/620.html) · [OWASP A07:2025](https://owasp.org/Top10/2025/A07_2025-Authentication_Failures/) · Verification items: [TC-AUTHN-05](#tc-authn-05)

<a id="t-019"></a><a id="f-019"></a>
#### F-019 · JWT verification does not pin the accepted signature

**Severity:** 🟠 High - verified attack-chain contributor in AC-T-005 (Authentication Bypass via Exposed Secret Material); see [§9](#9-abuse-cases)  ·  **Component:** [C-09](#c-09) - Authentication & Session Surface  ·  **Location:** `lib/insecurity.ts:52`

**Found by:** verification ([TC-AUTHN-02](#tc-authn-02))

**Reliability:** Medium. A model review confirmed it, but the survey and verification did not both find it.

**Weakness:** [W-003](#w-003) - Authentication relies on hand-rolled token verification

**Trust boundary gap:** [tb-2](#tb-2) 🌐 External - external → Authentication &amp; Session Surface · Authentication: The finding's evidence line is the interface this boundary cites; CWE-347 breaks its authentication condition.

**Issue:** An attacker crafts a token whose header names an algorithm other than `RS256`, such as `HS256` keyed with the public key, or none. `express-jwt`, `jwt.verify` and `jws.verify` are all called without a pinned algorithm, so the library's default handling decides whether the token is accepted.

An accepted token puts the attacker's chosen identity into `authenticatedUsers`.

**Evidence:** ✓ verified - Line 52 passes no algorithms option to `express-jwt`. Line 55 calls `jws.verify` without its algorithm argument, and line 189 calls `jwt.verify` without an algorithms option. Whether this is exploitable depends on library versions that were not served.

```typescript
  50 │ }
  51 │
→ 52 │ export const isAuthorized = () => expressJwt(({ secret: publicKey }) as any)
  53 │ export const denyAll = () => expressJwt({ secret: '' + Math.random() } as any)
  54 │ export const authorize = (user = {}) => jwt.sign(user, privateKey, { expiresIn: '6h', algorithm: 'RS256' })
```

**Fix:** Pin the signature algorithm explicitly and reject `alg:none` and unknown algorithms → ◕ [M-191](#m-191) — Pin RS256 in every token verification call

**Classification:** Broken Authentication · STRIDE: Spoofing · [CWE-347](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/347.html) · [OWASP A07:2025](https://owasp.org/Top10/2025/A07_2025-Authentication_Failures/) · Verification items: [TC-AUTHN-02](#tc-authn-02)

```typescript
→ 10 │ import { SecurityAnswerModel } from '../models/securityAnswer'
```

**Reliability:** High. Probably real: a model review confirmed it, and the survey and verification each found it.

**Weakness:** [W-013](#w-013) - Password reset relies on a guessable security answer without effective throttlin

**Issue:** An unauthenticated attacker who knows a victim's email sends POST `/rest/user/reset-password` with guessed or researched security answers and a new password. The attacker can also send repeated guesses to `/rest/user/login` for credential stuffing.

It is keyed on the client-supplied X-Forwarded-For header, so rotating that header allows unlimited guesses.

**Evidence:** ✓ verified - At line 41 a matching security answer alone authorizes the password update at line 44. No emailed token or second factor is required.

```typescript

  40 │       })
→ 41 │       if ((data != null) && security.hmac(answer) === data.answer) {
  42 │         const user = await UserModel.findByPk(data.UserId)
  43 │         if (user) {
```

**Fix:** ◕ [M-193](#m-193) — Replace question-based reset with an emailed single-use token

#### F-026 · Data erasure layout parameter selects an arbitrary

**Severity:** 🟠 High  ·  **Component:** [C-03](#c-03) - Juice Shop Express API  ·  **Location:** `routes/dataErasure.ts:104`

**Found by:** scanners and verification ([TC-FILE-01](#tc-file-01))

**Reliability:** Medium. A model review confirmed it, but the survey and verification did not both find it.

**Weakness:** [W-004](#w-004) - Input handling lacks enforced boundary validation

**Issue:** At `routes/dataErasure.ts:104` a logged-in user posts to `/dataerasure` with a layout value. `req.body` is spread into the render options, so the view engine loads the file the user names as the layout, and the first 100 characters of the rendered file come back in the response.

The controls are a deny-list that blocks only three path substrings (line 105) and a session cookie requirement (line 76).

**Evidence:** ✓ verified

```typescript
  102 │
  103 │       if (req.body.layout) {
→ 104 │         const filePath: string = path.resolve(req.body.layout).toLowerCase()
  105 │         const isForbiddenFile: boolean = (filePath.includes('ftp') || filePath.includes('ctf.key') || filePath.includes('encryptionkeys'))
  106 │         if (!isForbiddenFile) {
```

**Fix:** Resolve and normalise every constructed path and reject anything that escapes the intended base directory → ◕ [M-198](#m-198) — Constrain file paths to a safe base directory (🟠 [F-026](#f-026))

**Classification:** Insecure File Handling · STRIDE: Tampering · [CWE-22](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/22.html) · [OWASP A06:2025](https://owasp.org/Top10/2025/A06_2025-Insecure_Design/) · Verification items: [TC-FILE-01](#tc-file-01) · Rules: INJ-004

**Reliability:** Low. Not confirmed, this may be a false positive: Traversal check is weak and route is unauthenticated, but extraction runs only if `isChallengeEnabled(fileWriteChallenge)`, which is runtime configuration not shown.

**Weakness:** [W-004](#w-004) - Input handling lacks enforced boundary validation

**Issue:** An unauthenticated attacker uploads a .zip to POST `/file-upload` containing entries with parent-directory segments, such as '../..`/ftp/legal.md`' or a path into frontend assets. At `routes/fileUpload.ts:34`, `extractZipBuffer` resolves each entry and writes it.

Extracted entry sizes are not limited.

**Evidence:** ◌ ambiguous

```typescript

  33 │     if (absolutePath.includes(path.resolve('.'))) {
→ 34 │       await pipeline(entry.stream(), fs.createWriteStream('uploads/complaints/' + fileName))
  35 │     }
  36 │   }
```

**Fix:** Resolve and normalise every constructed path and reject anything that escapes the intended base directory → ◑ [M-167](#m-167) — Manual review: verify Path traversal in ZIP upload extraction writes outside the target… · ◕ [M-199](#m-199) — Confine extracted entries to the upload directory

**Classification:** Insecure File Handling · STRIDE: Tampering · [CWE-22](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/22.html) · [OWASP A06:2025](https://owasp.org/Top10/2025/A06_2025-Insecure_Design/) · Verification items: [TC-FILE-01](#tc-file-01), [TC-FILE-02](#tc-file-02)

<a id="t-028"></a><a id="f-028"></a>
#### F-028 · Basket item update not bound to the caller's basket

**Severity:** 🟠 High - verified attack-chain keystone in AC-B-001 (User reads or changes data of another user or tenant); see [§9](#9-abuse-cases)  ·  **Component:** [C-03](#c-03) - Juice Shop Express API  ·  **Location:** `routes/basketItems.ts:68`

**Found by:** scanners and verification ([TC-AUTHZ-01](#tc-authz-01))

**Reliability:** Medium. A model review confirmed it, but the survey and verification did not both find it.

**Weakness:** [W-005](#w-005) - Authorization is implemented route by route

**Issue:** An authenticated customer sends PUT `/api/BasketItems/:id` with the id of another customer's basket item. At `routes/basketItems.ts:68` the pre-update hook loads the item by id alone and calls `next()`, so the generated update changes the victim's basket item, for example its quantity.

Nothing compares `item.BasketId` with the caller's basket id.

**Evidence:** ✓ verified

```typescript
  66 │   return async (req: Request, res: Response, next: NextFunction) => {
  67 │     try {
→ 68 │       const item = await BasketItemModel.findOne({ where: { id: req.params.id } })
  69 │       const user = security.authenticatedUsers.from(req)
  70 │       challengeUtils.solveIf(challenges.basketManipulateChallenge, () => { return user && req.body.BasketId && user.bid != req.body.BasketId }) // eslint-disable-line eqeqeq
```

**Fix:** Tie every object lookup to the requesting user's identity and reject cross-tenant references → ◕ [M-200](#m-200) — Enforce object-level (ownership) authorization (🟠 [F-028](#f-028))

**Classification:** Broken Access Control · STRIDE: Tampering · [CWE-639](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/639.html) · [OWASP A01:2025](https://owasp.org/Top10/2025/A01_2025-Broken_Access_Control/) · Verification items: [TC-AUTHZ-01](#tc-authz-01) · Rules: AUTHZ-002

<a id="t-031"></a><a id="f-031"></a>
#### F-031 · NoSQL \$where injection

**Severity:** 🟠 High  ·  **Component:** [C-03](#c-03) - Juice Shop Express API  ·  **Location:** `routes/showProductReviews.ts:36`

**Found by:** survey and verification ([TC-INPUT-02](#tc-input-02))

**Reliability:** Low. Not confirmed, this may be a false positive: Sink is real, but the challenge-enabled setting and route authentication are not shown in supplied source; exploitation depends on runtime config.

**Weakness:** [W-008](#w-008) - Document-database queries take their structure from application values

**Issue:** An unauthenticated attacker calls GET `/rest/products/:id/reviews` with JavaScript in the id, such as `sleep(2000)` or 1||true. At `routes/showProductReviews.ts:36`, while the `noSqlCommand` challenge is enabled, the id is only truncated to 40 characters and then concatenated into a \$where predicate that the database evaluates as JavaScript.

A true expression returns the reviews of every product.

**Evidence:** ◌ ambiguous

```typescript
  34 │     const t0 = new Date().getTime()
  35 │
→ 36 │     db.reviewsCollection.find({ $where: 'this.product == ' + id }).then((reviews: Review[]) => {
  37 │       const t1 = new Date().getTime()
  38 │       challengeUtils.solveIf(challenges.noSqlCommandChallenge, () => { return (t1 - t0) > 2000 })
```

**Fix:** Replace string concatenation in query operators with parameter binding → ◑ [M-168](#m-168) — Manual review: verify NoSQL \$where injection through product id in review listing · ◕ [M-201](#m-201) — Query reviews by numeric product equality

**Classification:** Injection · STRIDE: Tampering · [CWE-943](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/943.html) · [OWASP A05:2025](https://owasp.org/Top10/2025/A05_2025-Injection/) · Verification items: [TC-INPUT-02](#tc-input-02)

**Trust boundary gap:** [tb-7](#tb-7) - Juice Shop Express API → external · Egress content: Unvalidated client messages are forwarded to the LLM provider, which breaks the assumption that only the intended user text and system prompt cross this boundary.

**Issue:** LLM01/ASI01: the handler passes `req.body.messages` straight into `streamText` as the conversation. It does not check roles or content, so a caller can add system or assistant turns and fabricated tool results. The route is unauthenticated.

<a id="t-047"></a><a id="f-047"></a>
#### F-047 · Passwords stored and compared as unsalted MD5

**Severity:** 🟠 High  ·  **Component:** [C-01](#c-01) - Sequelize application data access  ·  **Location:** Multiple locations (2)

**Found by:** survey, scanners and verification ([TC-AUTHN-04](#tc-authn-04), [TC-DATA-02](#tc-data-02))

**Reliability:** High. Probably real: a model review confirmed it, and the survey and verification each found it.

**Weakness:** [W-041](#w-041) - Security-sensitive data uses weak cryptographic primitives

**Instances (2):** `models/user.ts`: 76; `lib/insecurity.ts`: 41

**Issue:** At `models/user.ts:76`, the password setter calls `security.hash`, which at `lib/insecurity.ts:41` is a single unsalted `MD5` hex digest with no work factor. The same hash is used for login (`login.ts:34`) and for 2FA password confirmation (`2fa.ts:107`).

An attacker obtains the Users table from the SQLite database, for example through the login SQL injection, a user-returning endpoint, a database dump or a leaked file. Every stored password can be cracked offline, which allows account takeover and credential reuse against other services.

**Evidence:** ✓ verified - The password setter at `models/user.ts:76` stores `security.hash(clearTextPassword)`. That function is an unsalted `MD5` digest.

**Fix:** Replace the broken hash with a salted password-hashing function (bcrypt/Argon2id) → ◕ [M-216](#m-216) — Hash passwords with bcrypt or argon2id

**Classification:** Cryptographic Failures · STRIDE: Information Disclosure · [CWE-916](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/916.html) · [OWASP A04:2025](https://owasp.org/Top10/2025/A04_2025-Cryptographic_Failures/) · Verification items: [TC-AUTHN-04](#tc-authn-04), [TC-DATA-02](#tc-data-02), [TC-CRYPTO-01](#tc-crypto-01) · Rules: CRYPTO-001

**Evidence:** ✓ verified

